                                                                THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR
                                                              CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn

                                                  CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG


                                                 Abstract. We study the (n − 2)-plane Kakeya problem in Rn (n ≥ 4) subject to
                                                 convex Wolff axioms. In other words, we study finite families of δ-neighborhoods of
                                                 (n−2)-plane segments (δ-flakes) in Rn , with the property that not too many of them
arXiv:2610.10603v1 [math.CA] 7 Oct 2026




                                                 can be contained inside a common convex set. Our main result is that the union
                                                 of δ-flakes from such a set must have almost maximal volume. This can be viewed
                                                 as a higher-dimensional generalization of Wang and Zahl’s work for δ-tubes in R3 .
                                                 The latter implies the Kakeya set conjecture in R3 . As a consequence, we resolve
                                                 Rogers’ 2-plane Kakeya problem in R4 subject to certain curvature conditions on
                                                 two-dimensional families of direction subspaces in the Grassmannian Gr(4, 2).

                                        1. Introduction

  Let Gr(n, k) be the Grassmannian consisting of all k-subspaces (k-planes) in Rn ,
and let {t + V : t ∈ Rn , V ∈ Gr(n, k)} be the collection of all affine k-planes in Rn .
To avoid triviality, we will assume n ≥ 3 throughout the paper.
  A (δ, k)-flake is the δ-neighborhood of a unit cube in some affine k-plane. When
k = 2, we may simply call it a δ-plate. Suppose F is a set of (δ, k)-flakes in Rn . We
write
                                                            [
                                           U (F) :=             F.
                                                         F ∈F

The recent solution of the Kakeya conjecture in R3 by the groundbreaking work of
Wang and Zahl concerns |U (F)|, the volume of U (F), when n = 3 and k = 1. Roughly
speaking, if the distribution of F satisfies suitable nonconcentration conditions, then
|U (F)| is almost as large as |F||F |. In this paper, our goal is to show that the same
type of assumption also implies a lower bound for the volume of the union of flakes
of codimension 2 (i.e., k = n − 2).
  To state our main results, we need to first fix our terminology. We will write |F| for
the cardinality of F, and |F | for the volume of a (δ, k)-flake F ∈ F.1 For clarity, we
sometimes write Fδ instead of F to emphasize the scale. In this case, we may simply
say “δ-flake” instead of “(δ, n − 2)-flake”.
   As in [14], we will describe the nonconcentration conditions for convex sets as
follows. Suppose that W is a finite set of convex sets in Rn and suppose that K is
another convex set with |K| > 0. We define

                                 W[K] := {W ∈ W : W ⊂ K},

and
                                                            W ∈W[K] |W |
                                                        P
                                   ∆(W, K) :=                              .
                                                                |K|
The maximal density measuring how the convex sets in W cluster into coarser convex
sets is defined to be

                                 ∆max (W) := max ∆(W, K).
                                                   K convex

  For a set of convex sets W, we call a collection of Lebesgue measurable subsets
{Y (W ) : W ∈ W} a shading of W if Y (W ) ⊂ W for each W ∈ W. We define
                                                            [
                                     U (W, Y ) :=               Y (W ).
                                                        W ∈W


  1To make a distinction, we use ∥·∥ to denote the operator norm or the Euclidean norm.                      For
                                                  n−k
simplicity, we will often pretend that |F | = δ         for a (δ, k)-flake F , which is entirely harmless.
4            CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

If the shading Y is clear from the context, then we may also write U (W) for U (W, Y ).
We define the averaged density of the shading Y as
                                                  W ∈W |Y (W )|
                                                P
                                 λ(W, Y ) :=                      .
                                                    W ∈W |W |
                                                  P

For clarity, we also sometimes write YW and λ(YW ) in place of Y and λ(W, Y ).
    With these definitions, we can now state our main result:
Theorem 1.1 ((n − 2)-plane Kakeya in Rn ). For every ε > 0, there is some η > 0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently small δ > 0.
  If F is a set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn with ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η , and Y is a shading on F
with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η , then
                                     |U (F, Y )| ≥ δ ε |F||F |.

   Wang and Zahl [28, 26, 27] settled the case of Theorem 1.1 when n = 3. We record
it below for a comparison.
Theorem 1.2 (Kakeya set conjecture in R3 ). For every ε > 0, there is some η > 0
such that the following holds for all sufficiently small δ > 0.
  If T is a set of δ-tubes in B1 ⊂ R3 with ∆max (T) ≤ δ −η , and Y is a shading of T
with λ(T, Y ) ≥ δ η , then
                                     |U (T, Y )| ≥ δ ε |T||T |.
In particular, every Besicovitch set in R3 has Minkowski and Hausdorff dimension 3.

   It is not hard to see that, by a straightforward tensor-product argument, Theo-
rem 1.1 for n > 3 implies Theorem 1.2. Theorem 1.1 tells us that as long as the
“codimension” is 2, nonconcentration with respect to convex sets continues to be an
effective notion for the Kakeya problem. Furthermore, we notice that the analogous
problem of Theorem 1.1 for Gr(n, n − 1) has been resolved fairly easily in [27, Lemma
5.7]2 via a Córdoba-type L2 argument, which is standard in resolving the Kakeya
maximal conjecture in R2 (see [6]). Moreover, the special case for Gr(3, 2) was used
by Wang and Zahl in the same paper where they resolve the Kakeya conjecture in R3 .
This suggests that the resolution of the Kakeya conjecture in higher dimensions may
also benefit from studying analogous statements in Gr(n, k) for all 2 ≤ k ≤ n − 1.
Our Theorem 1.1 resolves the case when k = n − 2 without introducing any new
nonconcentration conditions.
   Polynomial Wolff axioms first introduced by Guth and Zahl [15] and later estab-
lished by Katz and Rogers [17] imply that direction-separated tubes cannot be con-
centrated nearby semialgebraic varieties. This nonconcentration condition is possibly
needed in higher-codimensional cases (i.e., 1 ≤ k < n − 2) (see [12, Section 9] for
some related comments). It might be surprising that the Kakeya set conjecture in
    2See Lemma 4.7 for the exact statement in our notation.
            THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                        5

R4 for sticky sets of tubes was recently proved by Wang and Zakharov [29] by only
imposing the convex Wolff axioms. So it would be interesting to see whether new
nonconcentration conditions are needed in non-sticky cases.
   Our result is undoubtedly related to different generalizations of higher-dimensional
Kakeya-Besicovitch sets. A natural one to recall here is the (n, k)-Besicovitch set,
which is a measure zero set in Rn such that it contains a translate of any subspaces
in Gr(n, k). Currently, it is still an open problem to decide if an (n, k)-Besicovitch
set exists for k > 1. Falconer [9] proved that no such set exists if k > n/2. Bourgain
[2] proved that no such set exists if

                                     2k−1 + k ≥ n.
                                             √
Oberlin [21] later extended the range to (1+ 2)k−1 +k > n. In particular, combining
the results of Falconer and Bourgain, (n, n − 2)-Besicovitch sets have been known to
have positive Lebesgue measure. However, this type of Besicovitch sets fails our
nonconcentration assumption in Theorem 1.1 whenever n ≥ 4. Indeed, a direct
counting shows that ∆max (F) ≳ δ 6−2n for any discretization of (n, n − 2)-Besicovitch
sets, where F is a (direction-separated) δ-discretized version of the (n − 2)-planes.

Remark 1.3. For more results on (n, k)-Besicovitch sets, we refer the reader to [20]
for their Hausdorff dimension, [10] for their Fourier dimension, and Mattila’s book
[18, Chapter 24] for a general exposition. It is worth mentioning that the k-plane
problem can also be formulated in terms of the dimension of the family in the affine
Grassmannian, see [11].

   Because of the non-existence conjecture, another higher-dimensional variant was
introduced by Rogers [24], which turns out to be more relevant to our study. More
precisely, he introduced a restricted variant of (n, 2)-Besicovitch sets, which only in-
volves translates of every member of some (n − 2)-dimensional “curved” submanifold
in Gr(n, 2). When n = 4, it was proved that such Kakeya-type sets have Hausdorff
dimension at least 7/2. In Section 17, we will show that the “curved” condition natu-
rally extends to Gr(n, n − 2), and indeed guarantees our nonconcentration condition
on the δ-flakes. Theorem 1.1 therefore resolves Rogers’ Kakeya problem for Gr(4, 2),
and actually Gr(n, n − 2) in general, i.e., such Kakeya-type sets have full Hausdorff
dimension (see Section 17 for the precise definitions and details).
  In fact, Rogers’ formulation also works for general k-planes, i.e., the direction
subspaces of them come from some (n − k)-dimensional submanifold M ⊂ Gr(n, k).
And a natural problem is to impose suitable conditions on M so that the k-planes are
essentially disjoint, i.e., we can expect full dimension for the Kakeya-type sets. So if
we are interested in the Hausdorff dimension of the Kakeya-type sets that can possibly
be Lebesgue measure zero, then the dimension complementarity k + dim M = n is in
some sense a more appropriate condition than considering the whole Grassmannian.
For (n, k)-Besicovitch sets, we are in the “supercritical” regime when k > 1 as k +
6          CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

dim Gr(n, k) > n. Recall from the last paragraph that our Theorem 1.1 provides a
satisfactory answer when k = n − 2.
   For general k, although we have seen the necessity of the dimension condition
dim M = n − k, it is still unclear what kind of curvature condition on M ⊂ Gr(n, k)
is suitable for Kakeya-type problems, and also for the stronger Kakeya maximal
estimate associated to M. A popular way to approach this is to make the additional
assumption that M is induced by the normal bundle of a surface Σ of codimension
k in Rn , which allows us to encode the curvature property of M by that of Σ. See
[8] for partial results on boundedness of the restricted 2-plane transform related to
“well-curved” quadratic surfaces of codimension 2. On the contrary, our main results
only provide information about dimension bounds.
  Finally, in view of the classical k = 1 case, it is reasonable to believe that the study
of restricted k-plane transforms, or even the restricted k-plane Kakeya sets, may
facilitate that of higher-codimensional Fourier restriction. We will not discuss this in
detail, but we hope to see progress in this direction in future work. Interested readers
may consult [3, Section 1] for references on Fourier restriction of higher-codimensional
quadratic surfaces, which is the most extensively investigated in the literature.

1.1. Overview of the Proof. Motivated by the work of Rogers [24], in Section 3,
we will introduce the concept of principal angles to study the intersection behavior of
the δ-flakes. This serves as the basic language to describe the overlapping patterns.
Our argument for Gr(n, n − 2) will be based on induction on n, whose base case
(n = 3) has been established by Theorem 1.2. However, as the geometry of δ-flakes in
Rn (n ≥ 4) is more complicated than that of δ-tubes in R3 , there are many additional
difficulties in executing the induction. In fact, we will need both the proof and the
statement of Theorem 1.2, combined with some new geometric ideas. In this sense,
our Theorem 1.1 should be regarded as a nontrivial extension of Theorem 1.2 instead
of a brand new way to prove it.
  From now on, we may assume n ≥ 4. Our proof strategy of Theorem 1.1 is to
divide the proof into sticky and non-sticky cases.
   We will study the sticky case first in this paper, as it contains most of the novel-
ties. One obvious technical obstacle is that Wang-Zahl’s proof of the sticky Kakeya
conjecture for tubes in R3 [28] relies heavily on existing deep results from projection
theory, whose higher-dimensional versions seem to be needed here but unfortunately
not yet systematically established in the literature. It is possible that one can still
pursue along the projection-theoretic approach with additional work, but we decided
to adopt a slicing approach to get around the technical projection theory parts.
   Our main idea and contribution here is to slice the δ-flakes by parallel affine 3-planes
in Rn , such that we have a set of δ-tubes satisfying assumptions in Theorem 1.2 inside
each of the slices. If this step is achievable, then we can simply apply Theorem 1.2
in each slice and then use a Fubini-type argument to finish the proof. The difficulty
is that we need to somehow find a way to relate the nonconcentration condition for
            THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                        7

δ-flakes in Rn to that for δ-tubes in each slice, which seems to be unclear in general
due to the complicated geometry. To overcome this difficulty, we will build a novel
induction scheme by applying a factoring lemma due to Wang and Zahl [27] inside each
slice. This would induce a medium grains structure in each slice, which, combined
with the local grains structure provides favourable rigidity. We hope that the scheme
initiated here will also be instructive to many other Kakeya-type problems where the
slicing philosophy potentially applies.
   Once the sticky case is settled, we will then reduce any general configurations of
the δ-flakes to the sticky case. To this end, we will mainly follow the streamlined
presentation in [14]. It turns out that there are not many additional difficulties in
adapting their argument to δ-flakes. However, due to the change of the dimensions,
the parameters in the argument need to be modified accordingly. Moreover, the mul-
tiscale decomposition and the two-ends reduction need to be clarified more carefully.
We decide to include the whole proof of the non-sticky part in this paper, so that
readers can identify the difference between our proof and the original proof in [14].
  As a historical side remark, the idea of general-to-sticky reduction was first system-
atically proposed by Katz and Tao [25], and it has been developed carefully by many
authors in other problems such as the Furstenberg sets conjecture [22, 23]. One may
consult [27, Section 1.4] and [12, Section 4] for a more detailed history. It was not
until recently that Wang and Zahl [27] successfully fleshed out the original Katz–Tao
program [25] and proved Theorem 1.2. It is unclear how much this methodology can
be adapted to other Kakeya-type problems, and our Theorem 1.1 is one step in this
direction.
   One may wonder whether the slicing argument can be applied directly to a general
set of δ-flakes (not necessarily sticky), so that there is no need to first reduce to
the sticky case and re-derive the proof in [14]. We do not know the answer. The
current proof relies heavily on the rigid graininess structure enjoyed by the sticky
case, similarly to what happens in [28]. Therefore, searching for a direct slicing
proof without the stickiness assumption would be very interesting. One additional
advantage of our approach is that Kakeya estimates for certain planks in Rn (satisfying
convex Wolff axioms) are also obtained as by-products of the proof of the general-to-
sticky reduction (Section 17.2). These estimates generalize Theorem 1.1 in a natural
way.




1.2. Organization of the paper. Sections 2–5 provide the preliminaries. Section 2
introduces notation and definitions, including uniformity (Section 2.4). Section 3
presents the principal-vector decomposition (Theorem 3.1) and develops geometric
8           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

lemmas for k-planes and (δ, k)-flakes. Section 4 introduces the Katz–Tao and Frost-
man nonconcentration conditions (Definitions 4.1 and 4.2), records the maximal den-
sity factoring lemma (Lemma 4.5), and gives a codimension-one analogue of Theo-
rem 1.1 (Lemma 4.7). Section 5 develops refinement lemmas compatible with fac-
toring and multiplicity estimates (Proposition 5.1 and Lemma 5.4). Readers familiar
with these preliminaries may skim these sections and return to them as needed.
   Sections 6–11 establish the sticky Kakeya theorem for δ-flakes (Theorem 6.2), with
the main new ingredients appearing in Sections 8–10. Section 6 introduces the rele-
vant notions of stickiness (Definitions 6.1 and 6.3) and formulates Theorem 6.2 and
its special case, Theorem 6.5. Section 7 sets up the extremal-family framework (Defi-
nitions 7.1 and 7.2) and establishes self-similarity properties (Proposition 7.5), which
will be used throughout the sticky argument. Section 8 proves the planiness and the
local grains structure (Proposition 8.4). The proof of Theorem 6.5 is then organized
into ten steps, outlined at the beginning of Section 9. Section 9 develops the slicing
argument that produces medium grains (Steps (i)–(vi)), while Section 10 combines
medium grains with local grains to complete the proof of Theorem 6.5 (Steps (vii)–
(x)). Section 11 then deduces Theorem 6.2 from Theorem 6.5.
   Sections 12–16 establish Theorem 1.1 through a reduction to the sticky case. Sec-
tion 12 introduces the assertions KKT (β) and KF (β) (Definitions 12.1 and 12.2) and
reduces Theorem 1.1 to Main Lemmas 1 and 2, whose iteration yields the desired
conclusion. A roadmap for their proofs appears immediately before Section 12.1.
Sections 13 and 14 are preparatory. Section 13 extends the multiplicity estimates to
planks (Lemmas 13.1 and 13.4), and to families of flakes factored by planks (Proposi-
tion 13.6). Section 14 establishes the multiscale decomposition (Lemma 14.5), which
provides the sticky and structured non-sticky alternatives used in the reduction. Sec-
tions 15 and 16 prove Main Lemmas 1 and 2, respectively, completing the proof of
Theorem 1.1.
   Section 17 presents applications of the main results. Section 17.1 applies Theo-
rem 1.1 to Rogers-type (n − 2)-plane Kakeya sets in Rn under the stated curvature
assumptions (Theorem 17.2), including Rogers’ 2-plane Kakeya problem in R4 . Sec-
tion 17.2 records the resulting Kakeya estimates for planks (Theorems 17.3 and 17.4).
Appendix A collects the probabilistic tools and proves Lemmas 11.6, 11.8 and 12.5.

                             2. Notation and Definitions

    Our convention basically follows that of [14].

2.1. Linear algebra. For a finite set of vectors {vj }kj=1 in Rn , we use span(v1 , . . . , vk )
to denote the linear space they span. For simplicity, if there is a single vector v ∈ Rn ,
then we define ⟨v⟩ := span(v). This should not be confused with inner products ⟨·, ·⟩.
    For 1 ≤ k1 ≤ k2 ≤ n and V ∈ Gr(n, k2 ), we define
                         Gr(V, k1 ) := {W ∈ Gr(n, k1 ) : W ⊂ V }.
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                           9

  Let πV be the orthogonal projection from Rn onto its subspace V . Let I be the
identity operator on Rn . Let |v1 ∧ v2 ∧ . . . ∧ vk | be the k-dimensional volume of the
parallelepiped generated by {vj }kj=1 ⊂ Rn (k ≤ n).

2.2. Convex sets and shadings. Given a family of convex sets V, we will often
                           P              P
abbreviate expressions like V ∈V f (V ) as V f (V ) for simplicity. Also, when all
convex sets in V have comparable volume, we will write |V | to denote the volume of
one of them.
    Let n ≥ 3. The dimensions of a convex set V ⊂ Rn are the lengths of the axes of
its inner John ellipsoid, listed in increasing order. We will denote this by v1 × v2 ×
· · · × vn . To ease notation, we do not distinguish comparable scales when writing
v1 × v2 × · · · × vn , which is harmless. For simplicity, we use the boldface vector “⃗1” to
denote the product of (n − 2) many “1”, and “v⃗1” to denote the product of (n − 2)
many “v”. A δ-tube is a convex set of dimension δ⃗1 × δ × 1. A δ-plate is a convex set
of dimension δ⃗1 × 1 × 1. A δ-flake is a convex set of dimension δ × δ × ⃗1. A slab is
a convex set of dimension θ × 1 × ⃗1 for some 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1. A plank is a convex set of
dimension a × b × ⃗1 for some 0 < a ≤ b ≤ 1. In general, we use Nr (E) to denote the
r-neighborhood of a set E ⊂ Rn .
   Let B n (x, r) denote a ball of radius r centered at x in Rn . For simplicity, we may
also use the notation Brn to denote a general ball of radius r in Rn , whose center is
either 0 or unimportant3. We omit the superscript n when it is clear from the context.
An additional convention is that B1 := B n (0, 1).
  Let C > 0 and let K be a symmetric convex body in Rn centered at x (i.e., if
x + y ∈ K for some y ∈ Rn , then x − y ∈ K). For example, K may be a tube, a flake,
a plank, a cube, or a ball. Then we use CK to denote the dilation of K with respect
to x by a factor C, i.e.,
                             CK := {y : (y − x)/C ∈ K}.
Here C is allowed to depend on δ (e.g., log(1/δ)), and x will often be implicit when
we write CK.
  In Section 9 and 10, due to more delicate geometry, we use the vector “⃗1” to denote
the product of (n − 3) many “1”, and “v⃗1” to denote the product of (n − 3) many “v”.
   A shading of a set V is a measurable set Y (V ) ⊂ V . We will use (V, Y ) to denote
a family of convex sets and their associated shadings {Y (V ) : V ∈ V}. For brevity,
we will sometimes say “let (V, Y ) be a family of convex subsets of Rn ”. Define the
multiplicity of a family of convex sets (W, Y ) as
                                                W ∈W |Y (W )|
                                              P
                                µ(W, Y ) :=                     .
                                                  |U (W, Y )|
When Y is clear, we may write µ(W) instead. Note that the conclusion of Theorem 1.1
can be equivalently replaced by µ(F) ≤ δ −ε .
  3It can be read from the context which of the two options holds.
10           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

   For x ∈ Rn , we define VY (x) := {V ∈ V : x ∈ Y (V )} and µ(V, Y )(x) := |VY (x)|.
We say that (V, Y ) has constant multiplicity if µ(V, Y )(x) is comparable for all x ∈
U (V, Y ). We say that (V′ , Y ′ ) is a refinement of (V, Y ) if V′ ⊂ V and Y ′ (V ) ⊂ Y (V )
for each V ∈ V′ . For c > 0, we say that (V′ , Y ′ ) is a c-refinement of (V, Y ) if
        ′                                                                                   ′
   V′ |Y (V )| ≥ c  V |Y (V )| additionally holds. Finally, if Y is a shading on V and V
P                  P

is a subset of V, then we will often abuse notation and continue to use Y to refer to
the restriction of the shading to V′ .

2.3. Pigeonholing and ⪅ notation. There are many different types of dyadic pi-
geonholing steps in the proof, and one may consult [14, Section 2.2] for a list of them.
These steps often involve harmless quantities of the form log(1/δ) or log |V|. Also, in
practice, any shading Y (V ) with small measure (i.e., |Y (V )| ≤ δ n ) can be replaced
by Y (V ) = ∅ without affecting our arguments. To streamline our arguments, if X
and Y are functions of a scale parameter ρ, then we use the notation “X ⪅ Y ” to
mean that for any ε > 0, there is a constant C = C(ε) such that X ≤ Cρ−ε Y . So ρ
will often be implicit. One can easily check that such notation reasonably applies to
all pigeonholing steps, see [14, Section 2.2] for details. Throughout the paper, unless
otherwise specified, we will use C to denote a constant ≲ 1, whose exact meaning
may vary from line to line.

2.4. Uniform sets of flakes. We say two flakes F, F ′ are essentially distinct if
|F ∩ F ′ | < c0 |F | for some fixed small constant 0 < c0 < 1/2, and a family of flakes is
essentially distinct if each pair of its elements is. It will be useful to consider δ-flakes
F that are uniform in the following sense.

Definition 2.1. Let δ > 0, and F be a set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn . For ρ ∈ [δ, 1], we
say that F is uniform at scale ρ if there is a set of ρ-flakes Fρ so that
               S
       (i) F = Fρ ∈Fρ F[Fρ ].
      (ii) The flakes Fρ are essentially distinct.
     (iii) |F[Fρ ]| is constant up to a factor ∼ 1 as Fρ varies in Fρ .
  Moreover, let M = ⌈log log(1/δ)⌉. We say that F is uniform if F is uniform at scale
ρk = δ k/M for each k = 0, . . . , M . In this case, we call the numbers Nk = |F[Fρk ]| the
branching numbers of F.

   Note that by definition, if F is uniform, then it must be essentially distinct. Item
(ii) above implies that each F ∈ F can only lie in Fρ for ∼ 1 choices of Fρ ∈ Fρ . For
F not necessarily uniform, we may still use Fρ to denote a set of ρ-flakes with the
smallest cardinality that satisfies Item (i) above. We may call Fρ a set of thickened
flakes at scale ρ.

Definition 2.2. Let δ > 0, M = ⌈log log(1/δ)⌉, and (F, Y ) be a set of δ-flakes in
B1 ⊂ Rn and their associated shading. We say that (F, Y ) is uniform if the following
holds:
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                         11

      • F is uniform.
      • For each point x ∈ U (F, Y ), the sets FY (x) are uniform, and have roughly the
        same branching numbers.

   The reason for setting M ∼ log log(1/δ) in our above definitions is as follows. First,
since (log |F|)log log(1/δ) ⪅ 1, by pigeonholing, if F is an essentially distinct family of
δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn , then it has a subset F′ of cardinality |F′ | ⪆ |F| that is uniform,
and every pair (F, Y ) has a ≈ 1-refinement that is uniform.
   Second, if F is uniform, then for every scale ρ ∈ [δ, 1], there is a set of ρ-flakes
Fρ that satisfies Item (i) and (ii) from Definition 2.1, and also the following slightly
weaker analogue of Item (iii): there is a number Nρ so that |F[Fρ ]| ≈ Nρ for all
Fρ ∈ Fρ . Similarly for Definition 2.2; the second bullet point is weakened to: the
branching numbers Nk are roughly the same up to a factor ≈ 1.
   In Section 11, we will extend the meaning of “uniform” so that certain convex Wolff
axiom constants are also uniformized (Proposition 11.1).

                              3. Geometry of k-planes

  In this section, we give some basic geometric lemmas for k-planes and (δ, k)-flakes in
 n
R , which will be used throughout the paper. In our proof of Theorem 1.1 (especially
the sticky case) we need to describe and analyze the overlapping pattern between two
k-planes. The main tool is called the principal-vector decomposition, which has been
systematically studied by many people, see [1, 16, 19, 30, 7].

3.1. Principal-vector decomposition and the major angle. Here we provide a
version of such a decomposition that will be useful for our purposes. It is not presented
in exactly the same form as any of the results in the references as mentioned, but is
indeed an organic combination of them.
Theorem 3.1 (principal-vector decomposition). For any V, W ∈ Gr(n, k), there exist
orthonormal bases
                  V := {v1 , . . . , vk } ⊂ V,   W := {w1 , . . . , wk } ⊂ W,
and a set of angles 0 ≤ θ1 ≤ . . . ≤ θk ≤ π/2, such that ⟨vi , wj ⟩ = δij cos θi , where
δij is the Kronecker delta. In particular, V and W correspond to the SVD (singular
value decomposition) of πW |V : V → W and πV |W : W → V , in the sense that
πW (vi ) = (cos θi )wi and πV (wi ) = (cos θi )vi for each i.
   We will call θ1 , . . . , θk the principal angles between V and W , and call V and W
principal vectors of V and W . The former is uniquely determined by V and W , while
the latter may not be. The whole structure is called a principal-vector decomposition,
which also enjoys the following properties:
      (i) ∥πV − πW ∥ = ∥(I − πW )|V ∥ = ∥(I − πV )|W ∥ = sin θk .
     (ii) If dim(V ∩ W ) = l, then θi = 0 for i ≤ l and θi > 0 for i > l. Moreover,
          |(vl+1 ∧ . . . ∧ vk ) ∧ (wl+1 ∧ . . . ∧ wk )| = sin θl+1 · · · sin θk .
12           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

     (iii) The nonzero principal angles between V ⊥ and W ⊥ are exactly the same as the
           nonzero principal angles between V and W .
We may write θi as θi (V, W ) to emphasize the subspaces.
Remark 3.2. Geometrically, the term ∥(I − πW )|V ∥ in Item (i) is the largest distance
from a point in V ∩ B1 to W , which is equal to the smallest ρ such that V ∩ B1 ⊂
Nρ (W ) ∩ B1 , or V ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (W ∩ B1 ). And similarly for ∥(I − πV )|W ∥. Therefore,
Item (i) means that if V ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (W ) ∩ B1 , then W ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ) ∩ B1 , and vice
versa. This observation allows us to describe the inclusion between (δ, k)-flakes at
different scales.
Definition 3.3. For a (δ, k)-flake F in Rn , we call the unique V ∈ Gr(n, k) parallel to
its defining affine k-plane the direction subspace of F , and we may write it as dir(F ).
Definition 3.4. In the setting of Theorem 3.1, we call ∠(V, W ) := sin(θk (V, W )) the
major angle between V and W . Given two (δ, k)-flakes F1 , F2 in Rn , we define their
major angle ∠(F1 , F2 ) by that between dir(F1 ) and dir(F2 ).
Remark 3.5. Since the operator norm ∥·∥ has the triangle inequality, we know from
Item (i) in Theorem 3.1 that ∠(V, W ) = ∥πV − πW ∥ is a metric on Gr(n, k), which is
a k(n − k)-dimensional smooth manifold. The normalized Hausdorff measure induced
by this metric is comparable to the normalized Haar measure/Riemannian measure
on Gr(n, k).
Lemma 3.6. If a (δ, k)-flake Fδ is contained in a (ρ, k)-flake Fρ for some ρ ∈ [δ, 1],
then ∠(Fδ , Fρ ) ≲ ρ. Also, if two (δ, k)-flakes F1 and F2 are contained in the same
(ρ, k)-flake, then ∠(F1 , F2 ) ≲ ρ. Conversely, if two (δ, k)-flakes F1 and F2 have
nontrivial intersection, then F1 is contained in the (ρ, k)-flake Nρ (F2 ) with ρ ≲
max{δ, ∠(F1 , F2 )}, and vice versa. All constants in these bounds depend only on
n.

   Lemma 3.6 is self-evident from Remark 3.2, and so we omit the proof. It is impor-
tant because it justifies many intuitions about the geometry of (δ, k)-flakes. In this
sense, the major angle can be used to quantify how “close” the direction subspaces of
flakes are and thus be a natural analogue of the classical angle between two lines/δ-
tubes in Rn . Also, Lemma 3.6 means that the major angle between two (δ, k)-flakes
is well defined up to error δ: In Definition 3.4, we can use any affine k-plane t + V
with t + V ∩ B1 ⊂ F to define the direction subspace of F . Roughly speaking, two
(δ, k)-flakes F1 and F2 in B1 ⊂ Rn are essentially distinct if either ∠(F1 , F2 ) ≳ δ, or
∠(F1 , F2 ) ≲ δ but the translation vector connecting their centers has component ≳ δ
in the common normal direction space.

3.2. Bilinear Kakeya-type estimate and the minor angle. The major angle is
not enough to accurately reflect all geometric information, such as the volume of the
intersection between two (δ, k)-flakes when k ≥ 2. This is a vital difference from the
case for δ-tubes. We need all principal angles.
              THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                                     13

     Lemma 3.7 has the following immediate corollary, which will be useful in Section 8:

   Intuitively, Lemma 3.7 means that for the volume of intersection to be small, we
need all principal angles to be large. Roughly speaking, if θj ≤ δ for some j, then
the intersection is large as it at least contains a (δ, j)-flake, which means the two
(δ, k)-flakes should be compressed into a common (δ, 2k − j)-flake. This intuition is
made rigorous by the following lemma:
Lemma 3.9. Suppose V, W ∈ Gr(n, k) have principal angles 0 ≤ θ1 ≤ . . . ≤ θk ≤ π/2
with θj ≤ ρ for some 2k − n ≤ j ≤ k and ρ ∈ [δ, 1].4 Then there exists some constant
C ≲ 1 and H ∈ Gr(n, 2k − j) such that NCρ (H) contains (Nδ (V ) ∪ Nδ (W )) ∩ B1 .
This H contains the internal bisector (line) of a pair of principal vectors defining θi
for each 1 ≤ i ≤ j. In general, if two (δ, k)-flakes intersect, then there exists some
constant C ≲ 1 and a (Cρ, 2k − j)-flake Fρ containing both of them.
     4Note that dim(V ∩ W ) ≥ 2k − n, so θ
                                                           j = 0 for all j ≤ 2k − n, and “j ≥ 2k − n” is a natural
requirement.
               THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                                               15

Proof. By translation invariance and Lemma 3.6, the second claim about flakes is an
immediate corollary of the first claim about subspaces. And so it suffices to prove
the first claim.
   Take a principal-vector decomposition of V and W as in Theorem 3.1. For each
1 ≤ i ≤ j, let hi := (vi + wi )/ ∥vi + wi ∥ (the unit vector along the internal bisector of
vi and wi ). Define

                            H ′ := span({hi }ji=1 , {vi }ki=j+1 , {wi }ki=j+1 ).

Then clearly dim H ′ ≤ 2k − j. Since 2k − j ≤ n, we can find some H ∈ Gr(n, 2k − j)
with H ′ ⊂ H. We need to verify that (Nδ (V ) ∪ Nδ (W )) ∩ B1 ⊂ NCρ (H). For this
purpose, note that for each 1 ≤ i ≤ j, since vi and wi make an angle θi ≤ ρ, we have
∥hi − vi ∥ = ∥hi − wi ∥ ≤ ρ, which implies that vi and wi both lie in Nρ (H). On the
other hand, it is clear that {vi }ki=j+1 , {wi }ki=j+1 ⊂ H ⊂ Nρ (H), and so V, W ⊂ Nρ (H),
which means that Nδ (V ) ∩ B1 ⊂ NCρ (H) and Nδ (W ) ∩ B1 ⊂ NCρ (H) for some C ≲ 1.
And so the proof is completed.                                                          □

  The final geometric notion we need is as follows:

Definition 3.10. In the setting of Theorem 3.1, if k ≥ 2, then we call ∠′ (V, W ) :=
sin(θk−1 (V, W )) the minor angle between V and W . Given two (δ, k)-flakes F1 , F2 in
Rn , we define their minor angle ∠′ (F1 , F2 ) by that between dir(F1 ) and dir(F2 ).

   In this paper, we care mostly about (n − 2)-planes (k = n − 2, n ≥ 4). Note that
for a pair of δ-flakes, θn−3 and θn−2 are the only possibly nonzero principal angles,
and their relative rotational position is completely determined by their major angle
and minor angle.

Remark 3.11. The major angle forms a metric by Remark 3.5, but the minor angle
does not. In [24], Rogers also considered major and minor angles in the study of
2-plane Kakeya (especially in R4 ), although his definition is superficially different
from Definition 3.4 and 3.10. More precisely, for a pair of δ-plates P1 , P2 in Rn with
dir(P1 ) = V and dir(P2 ) = W , he defines ∠(P1 , P2 ) as ∥πV − πW ∥, and ∠′ (P1 , P2 ) as

                                              min          ∥πℓ − πℓ′ ∥ .
                                           ℓ,ℓ′ ∈Gr(n,1)
                                            ℓ⊂V,ℓ′ ⊂W

However, by the structural properties of the principal-vector decomposition in Theo-
rem 3.1, the two definitions are actually exactly the same. Equivalence of ∠(P1 , P2 )
comes directly from Item (i) there. For ∠′ (P1 , P2 ), simply note that
                                                                      v
         min        ∥πℓ − πℓ′ ∥ =       min        sin(θ1 (ℓ, ℓ′ )) = u
                                                                      t1 −      max          cos(θ1 (ℓ, ℓ′ ))2
                                                                      u
       ′
     ℓ,ℓ ∈Gr(n,1)                     ′
                                    ℓ,ℓ ∈Gr(n,1)                             ℓ,ℓ′ ∈Gr(n,1)
      ℓ⊂V,ℓ′ ⊂W                      ℓ⊂V,ℓ′ ⊂W                                ℓ⊂V,ℓ′ ⊂W
                                    q
                               =        1 − cos(θ1 (V, W ))2 = sin(θ1 (V, W )).
16         CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

  Remark 3.11 is the historical reason why we have insisted on putting a “sin” in the
definition of major and minor angles, although sin θ ∼ θ whenever θ ∈ [0, π/2]. In
the (n − 2)-plane case (n ≥ 4), we can summarize what we get so far as follows:
     • By Item (i) in Theorem 3.1, ∥πV − πW ∥ = ∠(V, W ).
     • By Item (ii) in Theorem 3.1, |(vn−3 ∧vn−2 )∧(wn−3 ∧wn−2 )| = ∠′ (V, W )∠(V, W ).
       Note that this is true even if dim V ∩ W = n − 3, i.e., ∠′ (V, W ) = 0.
     • In Corollary 3.8, the Jacobian factor becomes
                  δ n−4 max{δ, ∠′ (F1,j1 , F2,j2 )} max{δ, ∠(F1,j1 , F2,j2 )}.
       This together with the prior bullet point recovers the special case of the mul-
       tilinear kj -plane estimate in [32, Theorem 1.5] when d = 4 and k1 = k2 = 2.
       However, our Corollary 3.8 does not necessarily require the two families to be
       “totally transverse”, which will be more flexible for our purposes.
     • In Lemma 3.9, we can roughly say that if ∠′ (F1 , F2 ) is small, then F1 and F2
       are compressed into a suitable neighborhood of an affine (n − 1)-plane.

3.3. Additional geometric lemmas. In Section 8–10, to analyze the sticky case,
we need more results on k-plane geometry. Still, only some special cases will be used,
but we keep the statements as general as possible for potential future applications.

Lemma 3.12. Let V, W ∈ Gr(n, k) with ∠(V, W ) ≤ 1/10, then Nδ (V ) ∩ Nδ (W ⊥ ) ⊂
B(0, 2δ) for all δ > 0. In particular, V ∩ W ⊥ = {0}.

Proof. Take x ∈ Nδ (V ) ∩ Nδ (W ⊥ ), then ∥πV ⊥ (x)∥ , ∥πW (x)∥ ≤ δ. By Remark 3.5, we
have ∥πV − πW ∥ = ∠(V, W ) ≤ 1/10, and so ∥πV (x) − πW (x)∥ ≤ (1/10) ∥x∥. Thus by
the Pythagorean theorem and triangle inequality, we get
                ∥x∥2 = ∥πV ⊥ (x)∥2 + ∥πV (x)∥2
                      ≤ ∥πV ⊥ (x)∥2 + (∥πW (x)∥ + ∥πV (x) − πW (x)∥)2
                      ≤ δ 2 + (δ + (1/10) ∥x∥)2 ,
                                         √
rearranging which yields ∥x∥ ≤ 10(1+99 199) δ < 2δ. So Nδ (V ) ∩ Nδ (W ⊥ ) ⊂ B(0, 2δ).
  Finally, if V ∩ W ⊥ ̸= {0}, then dim V ∩ W ⊥ ≥ 1, and so Nδ (V ∩ W ⊥ ) ⊂ Nδ (V ) ∩
Nδ (W ⊥ ) is unbounded, contradicting Nδ (V ) ∩ Nδ (W ⊥ ) ⊂ B(0, 2δ). Therefore, we
must have V ∩ W ⊥ = {0}.                                                           □

Lemma 3.13. Let 1 ≤ k1 ≤ k2 ≤ n, W ∈ Gr(n, k1 ), and V ∈ Gr(n, k2 ). Suppose
that W ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ) for some ρ ≤ 1/10, then dim πV (W ) = k1 , ∠(W, πV (W )) ≤ ρ,
and W ∩ V ⊥ = {0}. Moreover, V ⊥ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (W ⊥ ).

Proof. Clearly dim πV (W ) ≤ dim W = k1 . Suppose to the contrary that dim πV (W ) ≤
k1 − 1, then there exists a unit vector w0 ∈ W ∩ ker πV . Note that w0 ∈ W ∩ B1 ,
but w0 ̸∈ N1/10 (V ), contradicting the assumption that W ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ) (ρ ≤ 1/10).
Thus we must have dim πV (W ) = k1 .
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                            17

  Now for each w ∈ W ∩ B1 , we have πV (w) ∈ πV (W ) and ∥w − πV (w)∥ ≤ ρ, so
w ∈ Nρ (πV (W )). Thus W ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (πV (W )), which by Remark 3.2 implies that
∠(W, πV (W )) ≤ ρ.
   Since ∠(W, πV (W )) ≤ ρ, by Lemma 3.12, we get W ∩ πV (W )⊥ = {0}. As πV (W ) ⊂
V , we must have W ∩ V ⊥ = {0}.
   Finally, by Item (iii) of Theorem 3.1, we have ∠(W ⊥ , πV (W )⊥ ) = ∠(W, πV (W )) ≤
ρ, which by Remark 3.2 implies πV (W )⊥ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (W ⊥ ). Note that πV (W ) ⊂ V ,
and so V ⊥ ∩ B1 ⊂ πV (W )⊥ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (W ⊥ ), as desired.                             □
Lemma 3.14. Let 1 ≤ k1 ≤ k2 ≤ n, W ∈ Gr(n, k1 ), and V ∈ Gr(n, k2 ). Suppose
that W ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/10 (V ), then dim(V ∩ W ⊥ ) = k2 − k1 , V = πV (W ) ⊕ (V ∩ W ⊥ ),
and Nδ (V ) ∩ Nδ (W ⊥ ) ⊂ N2δ (V ∩ W ⊥ ) for all δ > 0.

Proof. By Lemma 3.13, we have dim πV (W ) = k1 and ∠(W, πV (W )) ≤ 1/10. Suppose
πV (W ) ∩ W ⊥ ̸= {0}, then there exists a unit vector v0 ∈ πV (W ) ∩ W ⊥ . Note
that v0 ∈ πV (W ) ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/10 (W ) by Remark 3.2, contradicting the fact that
v0 ∈ W ⊥ ∩ Sn−1 . Therefore, we must have πV (W ) ∩ (V ∩ W ⊥ ) = {0}, which implies
                    dim(V ∩ W ⊥ ) ≤ dim V − dim πV (W ) = k2 − k1 .
On the other hand,
     dim(V ∩ W ⊥ ) ≥ dim V + dim W ⊥ − dim Rn = k2 + (n − k1 ) − n = k2 − k1 ,
so we must have dim(V ∩ W ⊥ ) = k2 − k1 and V = πV (W ) ⊕ (V ∩ W ⊥ ).
  Now we can project everything along V ∩ W ⊥ to the (n − k2 + k1 )-plane (V ∩ W ⊥ )⊥ ,
and it remains to show the following claim: Let V ′ , W ′ ∈ Gr(n − k2 + k1 , k1 ) and
suppose that ∠(V ′ , W ′ ) ≤ 1/10, then Nδ (V ′ ) ∩ Nδ (W ′⊥ ) ⊂ B(0, 2δ). By Lemma 3.12,
the claim immediately holds true.                                                      □
Lemma 3.15. For i = 1, 2, let Vi , Vi′ ∈ Gr(n, ki ) with ∠(Vi , Vi′ ) ≤ ρ, where ρ ≤
1/100. Suppose that V1 ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/20 (V2⊥ ). Then V1 + V2 and V1′ + V2′ are both direct
sums, and (V1′ ⊕ V2′ ) ∩ B1 ⊂ N3ρ (V1 ⊕ V2 ). In particular, ∠(V1 ⊕ V2 , V1′ ⊕ V2′ ) ≤ 3ρ.

Proof. By Item (iii) of Theorem 3.1, we know that ∠(V2⊥ , V2′⊥ ) = ∠(V2 , V2′ ) ≤ ρ. So
by Remark 3.2, we have
  V1′ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V1 ∩ B1 ) ⊂ Nρ+1/20 (V2⊥ ∩ B1 ) ⊂ N2ρ+1/20 (V2′⊥ ∩ B1 ) ⊂ N1/10 (V2′⊥ ).
Thus Lemma 3.13 implies V1 ∩ V2 = {0} and V1′ ∩ V2′ = {0}. Therefore, we can
define V = V1 ⊕ V2 and V ′ = V1′ ⊕ V2′ , which satisfy dim V = dim V ′ = k1 + k2 . By
Remark 3.2, it remains to show that V ′ ∩ B1 ⊂ N3ρ (V ).
   For each unit vector v0 ∈ V2′⊥ ∩ V ′ , since V ′ = V1′ ⊕ V2′ , there exist unique v1 ∈ V1′
and v2 ∈ V2′ such that v0 = v1 + v2 . We claim that ∥v1 ∥ < 1.2 and ∥v2 ∥ < 0.12.
Note that v1 = v0 + (−v2 ) is subject to the orthogonal direct sum V ′ = (V2′⊥ ∩
V ′ ) ⊕ V2′ , we must have v1 ̸= 0. Also, by taking inner products on both sides with
v2 , we get |⟨v1 , v2 ⟩| = ∥v2 ∥2 . The key observation is that V1′ ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/10 (V2′⊥ )
18             CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

implies πV2′ (v1 / ∥v1 ∥) ≤ 1/10, which in turn implies |⟨v1 , v2 ⟩| ≤ ∥v2 ∥ πV2′ (v1 ) ≤
(1/10) ∥v1 ∥ ∥v2 ∥. If v2 ̸= 0, then by |⟨v1 , v2 ⟩| = ∥v2 ∥2 , we get ∥v2 ∥ ≤ (1/10) ∥v1 ∥.
Thus ∥v1 ∥ ≤ ∥v0 ∥ + ∥v2 ∥ ≤ 1 + (1/10) ∥v1 ∥, which implies ∥v1 ∥ ≤ 10/9 < 1.2 and
∥v2 ∥ ≤ (1/10) ∥v1 ∥ < 0.12. On the other hand if v2 = 0, then ∥v1 ∥ = ∥v0 ∥ = 1. So
the claim always holds true.
  We know that v0 = ∥v1 ∥ (v1 / ∥v1 ∥) + ∥v2 ∥ (v2 / ∥v2 ∥) with v1 / ∥v1 ∥ ∈ V1′ ∩ B1 ⊂
Nρ (V1 ) ⊂ Nρ (V ) and v2 / ∥v2 ∥ ∈ V2′ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V2 ) ⊂ Nρ (V ). So by linearity, we have
     ∥πV ⊥ (v0 )∥ ≤ ∥v1 ∥ ∥πV ⊥ (v1 / ∥v1 ∥)∥ + ∥v2 ∥ ∥πV ⊥ (v2 / ∥v2 ∥)∥ < 1.2ρ + 0.12ρ < 1.4ρ.

   Now for each unit vector v ∈ V ′ , since V ′ = (V2′⊥ ∩ V ′ ) ⊕ V2′ , there exist unique
 ′
v1 ∈ V2′⊥ ∩ V ′ and v2′ ∈ V2′ such that v = v1′ + v2′ . By orthogonality, we must have
∥v1′ ∥ , ∥v2′ ∥ ≤ ∥v∥ = 1. Writing v = ∥v1′ ∥ (v1′ / ∥v1′ ∥) + ∥v2′ ∥ (v2′ / ∥v2′ ∥) and taking the
v0 above as v1′ / ∥v1′ ∥, we obtain
            ∥πV ⊥ (v)∥ ≤ ∥v1′ ∥ ∥πV ⊥ (v0 )∥ + ∥v2′ ∥ ∥πV ⊥ (v2′ / ∥v2′ ∥)∥ < 1.4ρ + ρ < 3ρ.
Therefore, we can conclude that V ′ ∩ B1 ⊂ N3ρ (V ), as desired.                                   □
Lemma 3.16. For i = 1, 2, let Vi ∈ Gr(n, ki ) and Vi′ ∈ Gr(n, ki′ ) with ki′ ≤ ki and
Vi′ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (Vi ), where ρ ≤ 1/100. Suppose that V1 ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/20 (V2⊥ ). Then V1 + V2
and V1′ + V2′ are direct sums, and (V1′ ⊕ V2′ ) ∩ B1 ⊂ N3ρ (V1 ⊕ V2 ).

Proof. By applying Lemma 3.13 to V1 and V2⊥ , we know that V1 ∩ V2 = {0}, which
means V1 +V2 = V1 ⊕V2 . For i = 1, 2, let V̄i′ := πVi (Vi′ ), then by applying Lemma 3.13
again to Vi′ and Vi , we must have dim V̄i′ = ki′ and ∠(V̄i′ , Vi′ ) ≤ ρ. Note that V̄1′ ∩B1 ⊂
V1 ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/20 (V2⊥ ) ⊂ N1/20 (V̄2′⊥ ). So we can apply Lemma 3.15 to conclude
that V̄1′ + V̄2′ and V1′ + V2′ are direct sums, and (V1′ ⊕ V2′ ) ∩ B1 ⊂ N3ρ (V̄1′ ⊕ V̄2′ ) ⊂
N3ρ (V1 ⊕ V2 ).                                                                              □
Lemma 3.17. Let V ∈ Gr(n, k) and W ∈ Gr(n, n − k) satisfy ∠(V, W ⊥ ) ≤ ρ for
some ρ ≤ 1/100. Then for any ℓ ∈ Gr(n, 1) with ℓ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ), we have W + ℓ
intersects V at a unique line ℓ′ . Moreover, ∠(ℓ, ℓ′ ) ≤ 2ρ.

Proof. By Lemma 3.12, ∠(V, W ⊥ ) ≤ ρ ≤ 1/10 implies V ∩ W = {0}, and so Rn =
V ⊕W . By Remark 3.2, we know that ℓ∩B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ∩B1 ) ⊂ N2ρ (W ⊥ ) ⊂ N1/10 (W ⊥ ),
which by Lemma 3.13 implies ℓ ∩ W = {0}. Thus dim(W + ℓ) = n − k + 1, and so
dim(W + ℓ) ∩ V = dim(W + ℓ) + dim V − dim(W + ℓ + V ) = (n − k + 1) + k − n = 1.
This justifies the first claim.
  It remains to prove ∠(ℓ, ℓ′ ) ≤ 2ρ. Fix a unit vector v0 ∈ ℓ∩Sn−1 . Since Rn = V ⊕W ,
we have a unique decomposition v0 = v1 + w1 with v1 ∈ V and w1 ∈ W . If w1 = 0,
then v0 = v1 ∈ V , which means ℓ = ℓ′ ⊂ V , and so ∠(ℓ, ℓ′ ) = 0 < ρ. Thus we
may assume w1 ̸= 0. Since Rn = V ⊕ V ⊥ , we can further uniquely decompose w1 as
w1 = v2 + v3 with v2 ∈ V and v3 ∈ V ⊥ . Note that v0 = (v1 + v2 ) + v3 with v1 + v2 ∈ V
and v3 ∈ V ⊥ , and so it must be the unique decomposition of v0 under Rn = V ⊕ V ⊥ .
            THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                        19

In particular, as v0 ∈ ℓ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ), we get ∥v3 ∥ = ∥πV ⊥ (v0 )∥ ≤ ρ by definition of
neighborhoods.
   By Item (iii) of Theorem 3.1, we have ∠(V ⊥ , W ) = ∠(V, W ⊥ ) ≤ ρ, which by
Remark 3.2 implies W ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (V ⊥ ). In particular, as w1 / ∥w1 ∥ ∈ W ∩ B1 ⊂
Nρ (V ⊥ ), we get ∥v2 ∥ = ∥πV (w1 )∥ = ∥w1 ∥ · ∥πV (w1 / ∥w1 ∥)∥ ≤ ρ ∥w1 ∥ by definition
of neighborhoods. Now by the triangle inequality, we get ∥w1 ∥ ≤ ∥v2 ∥ + ∥v3 ∥ ≤
ρ ∥w1 ∥ + ρ, and so ∥w1 ∥ ≤ ρ/(1 − ρ) < 2ρ. Also, ∥v1 ∥ > ∥v0 ∥ − ∥w1 ∥ > 1 − 2ρ > 0,
which means v1 ̸= 0. Note that ℓ = ⟨v0 ⟩ and ℓ′ = ⟨v1 ⟩. Therefore, we obtain
∠(ℓ, ℓ′ ) = dist(v0 , ℓ′ ) = minv∈ℓ′ ∥v0 − v∥ ≤ ∥v0 − v1 ∥ = ∥w1 ∥ < 2ρ, as desired.   □

                            4. Organizing convex sets

   In this section, we introduce and analyze two types of nonconcentration conditions
for convex sets, called Katz–Tao conditions and Frostman conditions. We record
them from [14, Section 3] for the reader’s convenience.
Definition 4.1. A family of convex sets V is called C-convex-Katz–Tao (or C-Katz–
Tao for short) if ∆max (V) ≤ C. Informally, we say V is Katz–Tao if ∆max (V) ⪅ 1.
Definition 4.2. If V is a set of convex sets in another convex set K, then we define
                                          supK ′ ⊂K ∆(V, K ′ )
                           CF (V, K) :=                        ,
                                              ∆(V, K)
where the supremum is taken over all convex subsets of K. Note that we may equiv-
alently replace supK ′ ⊂K ∆(V, K ′ ) with ∆max (V) in the numerator.
  We say that V is C-convex-Frostman (or C-Frostman for short) in K if CF (V, K) ≤
C. Informally, we say that V is Frostman in K if CF (V, K) ⪅ 1. For simplicity, if V
has the form W[K] for some W, then we abbreviate CF (W[K], K) as CF (W[K]). In
particular, if K = B1 (thus V = V[K] trivially holds), then we abbreviate CF (V, K)
as CF (V), and we say that V is C-Frostman.

  The two conditions enjoy nice heredity properties amenable to induction on scales.
This is basically [14, Remark 3.3], and we record them here for the reader’s conve-
nience.
Proposition 4.3 (heredity properties).
                                                                              F
   (A) The property of being Frostman is inherited upwards: Suppose V = W VW is
       C-Frostman inside K, where each W ∈ W is contained in K and V ⊂ W for
       each V ∈ VW . Then there is a subset W′ ⊂ W that is ⪅ C-Frostman in K.
       If in addition the quantity VW |V |/|W | is approximately the same for each
                                  P

       W ∈ W, then we may take W′ = W, and in fact W is O(C)-Frostman in K.
   (B) The property of being Katz–Tao is inherited downwards: If V is C-Katz–Tao,
       then every subset V′ ⊂ V is also C-Katz–Tao.

  One may consult [14, Section 3] for a detailed proof of Proposition 4.3.
20           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

                                                             VW |V |/|W | is constant up to
                                                           P
Remark 4.4. In Proposition 4.3(A), if we assume that
a factor ≤ C̄ instead, then W is C̄C-Frostman in K.

  If V is a family of convex sets with ∆max (V) ≫ 1, it turns out to be useful to
consider the convex sets W so that ∆(V, W ) is near maximal. These convex sets W
can be used to organize V. A key tool in this philosophy is a factoring lemma first
proved by Wang and Zahl [27, Proposition 4.6]. Here we record the version in [14],
and one can find a detailed proof therein.
Lemma 4.5 (Maximal density factoring lemma, [14, Lemma 4.1]). Let V be a finite
family of convex subsets of Rn . Then there is a set V′ ⊂ V with V′ |V | ⪆ V |V |;
                                                                 P         P

a family W of convex sets in Rn , each of approximately the same dimensions; and a
partition
                                      V′ =
                                              G
                                                    VW ,
                                             W ∈W

where each set VW is contained in W . This partition has the following properties.
      (i) W is Katz–Tao, i.e. ∆max (W) ∼ 1.
     (ii) VW is Frostman in W , i.e. ∆(VW , W ) ∼ ∆max (VW ) for each W ∈ W.
          Furthermore, ∆(VW , W ) ∼ ∆max (V′ ).
Definition 4.6. Let V and W be families of convex subsets of Rn , and suppose there
                  F
is a partition V = W VW . We say that W factors V if the following is true:
        • W is Katz–Tao, i.e. ∆max (W) ∼ 1.
        • VW is Frostman in W , i.e. ∆(VW , W ) ∼ ∆max (VW ) for each W ∈ W. Fur-
          thermore, ∆(VW , W ) ∼ ∆max (V).
        • Each W ∈ W has approximately the same dimensions, and each set in VW is
          contained in W .

  Thus Lemma 4.5 says that W factors V′ . We call the set W coming from Lemma 4.5
the maximal density factoring of V. Thanks to the factoring lemma, we can often
reduce the problem of understanding V to the problem of understanding the Frostman
sets V[W ] for W ∈ W and the problem of understanding the Katz–Tao set W. This
will facilitate certain unusual induction scheme and allows reduction to the sticky
case. Wang and Zahl [27] successfully carried it out for δ-tubes in R3 , while we care
about general δ-flakes in Rn .
  We end this section with the Gr(n, n−1) version of Theorem 1.1, which is essentially
[27, Lemma 5.7], but is recorded here for the reader’s convenience:
Lemma 4.7. Suppose S is a set of δ × 1 × ⃗1 slabs in B1 ⊂ Rn (not necessarily
essentially distinct) with a shading Y . Then
(4.1)               |U (S, Y )| ≳ log(2/δ)−1 ∆max (S)−1 λ(S, Y )2 |S||S|.
Equivalently, we have
(4.2)                   |U (S, Y )| ≳ log(2/δ)−1 CF (S)−1 λ(S, Y )2 .
            THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                          21

Proof. The first inequality (4.1) follows immediately from [27, Lemma 5.7] (which
relies on a simple Córdoba-type L2 argument) by noting that “λ” therein corresponds
to λ(S, Y ) here, while “m = CKT −CW (S)” therein corresponds to ∆max (S) here. The
second inequality (4.2) is clearly equivalent to (4.1), as CF (S) ∼ ∆max (S)/(|S||S|) by
Definition 4.2.                                                                       □

  Lemma 4.7 will be used in many places throughout the paper.

[14, Section 5] for motivations, intuitions, and remarks. We will generalize things to
δ-flakes in Rn .

Remark 5.5. In Lemma 5.4, if |F[Fρ ]| is constant up to a factor ≈ 1 for all Fρ ∈ Fρ ,
then we can take F′ = F and F′ρ = Fρ . In other words, we can just refine the shadings,
while keeping the original set of flakes unchanged. This can be easily seen from the
proof of [14, Proposition 5.1].

                           6. Sticky Kakeya for flakes

   Now we are in the right place to formally start the proof of Theorem 1.1, which is
set up by induction on the dimension n. By Theorem 1.2, the n = 3 case is already
known, which serves as the base case. From now on, we always assume n ≥ 4, and it
suffices to prove Theorem 1.1 under the induction hypothesis that it holds when n is
replaced with n − 1. Throughout the proof, this hypothesis is only used at one place,
i.e., Step (ii) in Section 9.
  Let us begin with the sticky case. In this section, we will introduce versions of the
(n − 2)-plane sticky Kakeya theorem, and indicate the relationship between them.
  Let F be a uniform set of δ-flakes. Let ρ ∈ [δ, 1] and let Fρ be the thickening of F
at scale ρ. We begin with the following two observations:
     (A) If F is Frostman, then by Proposition 4.3(A), Fρ is Frostman. In general,
         however, the flakes in F[Fρ ] will not be Frostman inside Fρ .
     (B) If F is Katz–Tao, then by Proposition 4.3(B), the flakes in F[Fρ ] will be Katz–
         Tao. In general, however, the flakes in Fρ will not be Katz–Tao.
  It will be helpful to consider the special cases where the sets F[Fρ ] are Frostman in
Fρ , or the set Fρ is Katz–Tao.
Definition 6.1. Let F be a set of δ-flakes in Rn .
     (A) We say that F is C Frostman at every scale if for every scale δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, we
         have
                          CF (F[Fρ ]) ≤ C for every Fρ ∈ Fρ .
     (B) We say that F is C Katz–Tao at every scale if for every scale δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, we
         have
                                  ∆max (Fρ ) ≤ C.

  We will usually consider sets of flakes that are uniform, in the sense of Definition 2.1,
but this is not required in the above definition.
  We say that F is sticky if it is both Frostman at every scale and Katz–Tao at every
scale. In that case, we expect an (n − 2)-plane sticky Kakeya theorem, which says
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                         25

that |U (F)| ⪆ 1 and µ(F) ⪅ 1. A mild generalization says that if F is Frostman at
every scale, then |U (F)| ⪆ 1; while if F is Katz–Tao at every scale, then µ(F) ⪅ 1.
The precise statement is as follows:
Theorem 6.2. For all ε > 0, there exists η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for
all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn , with
λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
   (A) If F is δ −η Frostman at every scale, then
                                         |U (F, Y )| ≥ δ ε .
   (B) If F is δ −η Katz–Tao at every scale, then
                                         µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε .

   Similarly to Theorem 1.1, by a straightforward tensor-product argument, the spe-
cial case when n = 3 solved by Wang and Zahl [28, 26, 27] can be deduced from
Theorem 6.2. However, the geometry of δ-flakes is more complicated than δ-tubes
due to multiple principal angles (see Section 3), and so Theorem 6.2 does not come
immediately from Wang and Zahl’s work.
  We will first prove Theorem 6.2 in the special case when |F| ≈ δ −2 , under which
Parts (A) and (B) are essentially equivalent. This motivates the following stronger
version of stickiness:
Definition 6.3. We say that a set of δ-flakes F in B1 ⊂ Rn is δ −ε -sticky if
   (a) F is essentially distinct.
   (b) ∆max (F) ≤ δ −ε .
   (c) For each δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1 and Fρ ∈ Fρ , we have
                                   2                          2
                                   ρ                            ρ
                             δε          ≤ |F[Fρ ]| ≤ δ −ε            .
                                   δ                            δ
Remark 6.4. Item (c) implies δ −2+ε ≤ |F| ≤ δ −2−ε , and so for each δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, we have
δ 2ε ρ−2 ≤ |Fρ | ≤ δ −2ε ρ−2 . Item (b) implies ∆max (F[Fρ ]) ≤ δ −ε by Proposition 4.3(B).
However, Item (c) also implies δ ε ≤ ∆(F[Fρ ], Fρ ) ≤ δ −ε , and so CF (F[Fρ ]) ≤ δ −2ε ,
i.e., F is δ −2ε Frostman at every scale.

  We know from Remark 6.4 that Theorem 6.2, once established, should immediately
imply:
Theorem 6.5. For all ε > 0, there exists η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all
δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be a set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn such that F is δ −η -sticky and
λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . Then
                                       |U (F, Y )| ≥ δ ε .
Remark 6.6. Standard pigeonholing arguments allows us to only consider Theorem 6.5
in the case when (F, Y ) is uniform.
26           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

  In Section 11, we will show that the converse is also true, i.e., Theorem 6.5 actually
implies Theorem 6.2. More precisely, we will first show that Part (A) of Theorem 6.2
implies Part (B), and then show that Theorem 6.5 implies Part (A). These arguments
are kind of standard and only relies on certain probabilistic methods, so we choose
to postpone them until later on. Therefore, for the following few sections (Section 7–
10), we will focus on how to prove Theorem 6.5, presents the principal challenge and
embodies our key technical novelty.

          7. Extremal families of flakes and multiscale structure

  In this section, we will set up the induction framework for Theorem 6.5 and provide
some useful self-similarity properties. We will always work in Rn .
Definition 7.1. For ε, δ ∈ (0, 1), define
                                 M (ε, δ) = inf |U (F, Y )|.
                                             F,Y

Here, the infimum is taken over all sets of δ-flakes (F, Y ) with the following properties:
     (a) F is δ −ε -sticky.
     (b) λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ ε .
Next, let
                                               log M (ε, δ)
                               N (ε) = lim sup              ,
                                         δ→0+      log δ
which is defined for all ε ∈ (0, 1). Observe that for each δ ∈ (0, 1), if ε ≤ ε′ , then
M (ε, δ) ≥ M (ε′ , δ), and hence N (ε) ≤ N (ε′ ). In particular, since 0 ≤ N (ε) ≤ n, we
have that
                                 lim+ N (ε) = inf N (ε).
                                 ε→0           ε∈(0,1)
Denote this common value by σ.

  We may regard σ ≥ 0 as fixed from now on. Observe that σ = 0 immediately
implies Theorem 6.5. Also, since we only care about small ε in practice, we may
always assume that 0 < ε < 1/100. To prove σ = 0, we will start by assuming σ > 0
and study families of flakes that nearly extremize the quantity M from Definition 7.1
to get a contradiction.
Definition 7.2. Let ε, δ > 0. We say a set of δ-flakes (F, Y ) is ε-extremal if it satisfies
Items (a) and (b) from Definition 7.1, and furthermore |U (F, Y )| ≤ δ σ−ε .
Remark 7.3. Note that any ε-extremal set of δ-flakes has µ(F, Y ) ≥ δ −σ+3ε . On the
other hand, if we only assume (F, Y ) to satisfy Items (a) and (b), then for any given
ε0 > 0, by choosing ε small enough depending on ε0 , we have |U (F, Y )| ≥ δ σ+ε0
(thus µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −σ−2ε0 ) for small δ. Finally, if (F′ , Y ′ ) is a refinement of it with
                  ′
λ(F′ , Y ′ ) ≥ δ ε for some ε′ > ε, then (F, Y ′ ) is ε′ -extremal by letting Y ′ (F ) = ∅ for
F ∈ F \ F′ . And abusing notation, we may sometimes say that (F′ , Y ′ ) is ε′ -extremal.
This does not affect the arguments as U (F, Y ′ ) = U (F′ , Y ′ ).
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                                 27

   From the definition of σ, we immediately conclude that extremal collections of
flakes always exist for small δ. More precisely, we have the following.
Lemma 7.4. Let ε, δ0 > 0. Then there exists an ε-extremal set of δ-flakes (F, Y ) for
some δ ∈ (0, δ0 ).
   We can now state the main result of this section, which can be regarded as an
upgraded version of Lemma 5.4. It says that extremal collections of δ-flakes look
self-similar at all scales.
Proposition 7.5. For all ε > 0, there exist η > 0 and δ0 > 0 so that the following
holds for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes, and let ρ ∈
[δ 1−ε , δ ε ]. Then there is a ≈ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) of (F, Y ), a subset F′ρ ⊂ Fρ , and a
shading YF′ρ on F′ρ with λ(F′ρ , YF′ρ ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ), such that the following properties hold:
    (i) (F′ρ , YF′ρ ) is ε-extremal.
   (ii) For each x ∈ Rn , µ(F′ρ , YF′ρ )(x) ≤ ρ−σ−ε . In fact, on U (F′ρ , YF′ρ ), µ(F′ρ , YF′ρ )(x)
        is constant and satisfies
                              ρ−σ+ε ≤ µ(F′ρ , YF′ρ )(x) ≤ ρ−σ−ε .
   (iii) For each Fρ ∈ F′ρ and x ∈ Rn , µ(F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ )(x) ≤ (δ/ρ)−σ−ε . In fact, on
         U (F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ ), µ(F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ )(x) is constant and satisfies
                        (δ/ρ)−σ+ε ≤ µ(F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ )(x) ≤ (δ/ρ)−σ−ε .
        The constant is also the same for all Fρ ∈ F′ρ .
   (iv) F′ = F′ρ F′ [Fρ ], and for each Fρ ∈ F′ρ , U (F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ ) ⊂ YF′ρ (Fρ ).
              F

    (v) |U (F′ , Y ′ ) ∩ B(x, ρ)| is roughly the same for every x ∈ U (F′ρ , YF′ρ ). In other
        words,
                                                            |U (F′ , Y ′ ) ∩ B(x, ρ)|
                        |U (F′ , Y ′ )| ∼ |U (F′ρ , YF′ρ )|                           .
                                                                    |B(x, ρ)|
   By the convention in [28], we say that (F′ρ , YF′ρ ) covers (F′ , YF′ ) if Item (iv) above
holds, and call such a cover balanced if Item (v) additionally holds. The proof of
Proposition 7.5 is very similar to that for [28, Proposition 3.2] (see also [26, Proposi-
tion 6.2]). Here we provide an exposition using (the proof of) [14, Proposition 5.1].
Before that, we need some technical lemmas:

   In the proof of Proposition 7.5, YF′ρ is essentially induced by Y ′ , so we can further

             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                           33

                            8. Planiness and graininess

   In this section, we will use the geometric lemmas established in Section 3 to find
a plane map and then construct the local grains as in [28, Section 4]. The main
difficulty is that flakes have both major and minor angles, which makes the geometry
much more complicated than tubes, as we will see in the proof.
  Before that, let us first show a reduction about robust transversality: If (F, Y ) is
an extremal collection of flakes, then the direction subspaces of the flakes passing
through a typical point cannot focus too tightly. This can be viewed as a natural
analogue of [28, Lemma 3.4].

Lemma 8.1. For all ε > 0, there exist η > 0 and δ0 > 0 so that the following holds
for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes. Then there is a ≈ 1
refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) of (F, Y ) so that for each x ∈ Rn and V ∈ Gr(n, n − 2), we have
(8.1)                |{F ∈ F′Y ′ (x) : ∠(V, dir(F )) < δ ε }| ≤ δ −σ+εσ/2 .

Proof. Apply Proposition 7.5 with εσ/2 in place of ε, and ρ = δ ε . By Lemma 3.6,
the δ-flakes contributing to (8.1) are all contained in the ρ-flake Nρ (V ) + x. Since the
resulting set (F′ρ , YF′ρ ) of ρ-flakes is essentially distinct, these δ-flakes can be further
covered by O(1) ρ-flakes from F′ρ . Now by Item (iii) in Proposition 7.5, we can bound
the left hand side of (8.1) by
                                                                       2
                    µ(F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ )(x) ≤ (δ/ρ)−σ−εσ/2 = δ −σ+εσ/2+ε σ/2 ,
which yields the desired bound.                                                            □

   Roughly speaking, Lemma 8.1 allows us to always assume the major angles between
flakes to be ≈ 1. We will see the existence of extremal sets of flakes (F, Y ) with
additional structural properties, the first of which is the existence of a Lipschitz
plane map.

Definition 8.2. Let (F, Y ) be a set of δ-flakes. A plane map for (F, Y ) is a function
H : U (F, Y ) → Gr(n, 1) so that for each x ∈ U (F, Y ), we have
                    dir(F ) ∩ B1 ⊂ N2δ (H(x)⊥ ) for all F ∈ FY (x).

  We expect U (F, Y ) to arrange itself into a union of thin slab segments, which we
call local grains. They look like an Ahlfors-David regular set ([28, Definition 4.2]) in
the normal direction. We record the specific definition for the reader’s convenience:

Definition 8.3. For n ≥ 1, α ∈ [0, n], C ≥ 1 and δ > 0, we say a set E ⊂ Rn is a
(δ, α, C)n -ADset if for all x ∈ Rn , all ρ ≥ δ, and all r ≥ ρ, we have
                               Eρ (E ∩ B(x, r)) ≤ C(r/ρ)α .
Here Eρ (·) denotes the smallest number of ρ-balls needed to cover a set, which is
comparable to the largest number of ρ-separated points in a set.
34            CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

 One may consult [28, Remark 4.1] for some intuitive explanation of this definition.
We can now state the main result of this section.
Proposition 8.4. For all ε, δ0 > 0, there exist δ ∈ (0, δ0 ] and an ε-extremal set of
δ-flakes (F, Y ) with a 1-Lipschitz6 plane map H for it, such that for all ρ ∈ [δ, 1] and
x ∈ U (F, Y ), πH(x) (U (F, Y ) ∩ B(x, ρ1/2 )) is a (ρ, 1 − σ, δ −ε )1 -ADset7.
Remark 8.5. We do not know a priori whether 1 − σ ≥ 0 or not, but one can see
from the proof that this must be true. Intuitively, this is because we will use local
hairbrush-type arguments to grow grains somewhere in the proof, which roughly tells
that the “dimension” of the union is at least n − 1, and so σ ≤ 1.

  Roughly speaking, Proposition 8.4 implies that each Nρ (U (F, Y )) ∩ B(x, ρ1/2 ) is
contained in a union of at most ρσ/2−1/2 parallel rectangular slabs of dimensions
roughly ρ × ρ1/2 × ρ1/2⃗1. Since U (F, Y ) can be covered by δ −O(ε) ρσ/2−n/2 balls of
radius ρ1/2 , this implies that |Nρ (U (F, Y ))| ≲ δ −O(ε) ρσ . On the other hand, this
containment is nearly sharp, since |Nρ (U (F, Y ))| ≳ δ O(ε) ρσ .
Remark 8.6. Our Proposition 8.4 provides the so-called local grains structure for (n −
2)-plane Kakeya in Rn , whose statement is similar to that in [28, Proposition 4.1(2)],
but the proof here is more complicated, as one will soon see. It is conceivable that
a global grains structure similar to that in [28, Proposition 4.1(1)] could also be
established, but we will not need it for our purposes. In fact, we only need to apply
Proposition 8.4 at some special scales later on, but we think that it is beneficial to
present the full version of it, which does not incur additional difficulties.

   The remaining parts of this section will be devoted to the proof of Proposition 8.4,
which will be divided into three subsections. In Section 8.1, we will show that every
extremal set of flakes has a refinement with a plane map. In Section 8.2, we will
show that if an extremal set of flakes has a plane map, then after restricting to a
refinement, the plane map must be Lipschitz with controlled Lipschitz norm. In
Section 8.3, we will show that if an extremal set of flakes has a Lipschitz plane map,
then after restricting to a refinement, the flakes arrange themselves into local grains.


8.1. Finding a plane map. In this section, we will show that every extremal set
of flakes has a refinement with a plane map. Our main tools will be the bilinear
Kakeya-type estimate Corollary 3.8 proved in Section 3, as well as the multilinear
Kakeya estimate due to Carbery and Valdimarsson ([4, Theorem 1], see also [28,
Theorem 4.2]). We recall the former in the special case when k = n − 2, and the two
families coincide:
     6By Remark 3.5, the major angle yields a natural metric on Gr(n, 1).       And C-Lipschitz means
∠(H(x), H(y)) ≤ C ∥x − y∥ for all admissible pairs of x, y.
   7Here we are fixing an arbitrary isometry H(x) ∼ R to make sense of the terminology. Equiva-
                                                     =
lently, since H(x) ⊂ Rn , one can also just write “(ρ, 1 − σ, δ −ε )n -ADset” instead.

Corollary 8.7. Let F be a set of δ-flakes in Rn , then

   First, we prove that every extremal set of flakes has a refinement that “weakly”
agrees with a plane map. Here comes the difference from the tube case in [28]: If we
use Corollary 8.7 as in [28, Section 4.1], then we are no longer immediately reduced to
the plany case. Instead, there is an additional case when a generic point x in U (F, Y )
has all FY (x) sharing a common (n − 3)-plane through x. However, by using certain
slicing arguments and applying trilinear Kakeya for tubes in each slice, this additional
case can actually be further reduced to the plany case. Once the weak planiness has
been established, it is not hard to upgrade it to a strong one.
Lemma 8.8. There exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let
(F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes. Then at least one of the following cases holds
true:
    (i) There is a ⪆ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) and a function H : U (F′ , Y ′ ) → Gr(n, 1)
        such that for each x ∈ U (F′ , Y ′ ), we have
                  dir(F ) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/5 (H(x)⊥ )          for all F ∈ F′Y ′ (x).
   (ii) There is a ⪆ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) and a function X : U (F′ , Y ′ ) → Gr(n, n−3)
        such that for each x ∈ U (F′ , Y ′ ), we have
                   X (x) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/2 (dir(F ))         for all F ∈ F′Y ′ (x).

  The proof of this lemma relies on two additional ingredients, which we now intro-
duce. The first is about the maximum number of triangles sharing a common edge,
which is usually called the booksize of a graph. The version we present here is by no
means optimal, but suffices for our purposes.
Lemma 8.9. Let G be a simple graph with n vertices and m edges such that m ≥ λn2
for some λ > 1/4. Then there exists uv ∈ E (E is the set of edges in G) such that
the number of triangles containing uv is at least (4λ − 1)n.
Proof. Let N (x) be the set of neighbors of a vertex x in G. Let T be the number
of triangles in G, and for each uv ∈ E, let Q(uv) := |N (u) ∩ N (v)| be the number
of triangles containing uv ∈ E. Since each triangle is contributed by three sides, we
have
                                       X
                                             Q(uv) = 3T ,
                                      uv∈E

which guarantees the existence of an edge uv ∈ E with Q(uv) ≥ 3T /m.
  By a standard Goodman-type supersaturation inequality (see [33, Exercise 1.1.4]),
we have
                                     4m       n2
                                                
                                T ≥       m−      .
                                     3n        4
36           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

So we can conclude that
                                       3T   4    n2

                 max |N (u) ∩ N (v)| ≥    ≥   m−                 ≥ (4λ − 1)n,
                 uv∈E                  m    n    4
which is exactly what we want.                                                            □

   The second ingredient is geometric and basically says the following: If each pair of
three flakes intersects transversely nearby an affine (n − 3)-plane and the three result-
ing planes differ significantly, then they must be contained in a small neighborhood
of an affine (n − 1)-plane.

Lemma 8.10. Let σ > 0, and Vi ∈ Gr(n, n − 2) (i = 1, 2, 3) satisfy ∠(Vi , Vj ) ≥ δ σ/10
and ∠′ (Vi , Vj ) ≤ δ 9σ/10 (∀ {i, j} ⊂ {1, 2, 3}). Let H be the output (n − 1)-plane of
Lemma 3.9 for the pair V1 and V2 . Recall that H contains the internal bisector ℓ12 of
a pair of principal vectors defining ∠′ (V1 , V2 ). Let X12 := (V1 ∩ V2 ) + ℓ12 . For δ > 0
small depending on σ, if X12 ∩ B1 ̸⊂ Nδσ/2 (V3 ), then V3 ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/5 (H).

Proof. Similarly to X12 , we define X13 := (V1 ∩ V3 ) + ℓ13 , and X23 := (V2 ∩ V3 ) + ℓ23 ,
where ℓ13 and ℓ23 bisect ∠′ (V1 , V3 ) and ∠′ (V2 , V3 ) respectively. They are all (n − 3)-
planes.
     By our assumption of minor angles, we have

                X13 ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδ9σ/10 (V1 ) ∩ Nδ9σ/10 (V3 ) ∩ B1 ⊂ NCδ9σ/10 (H),

and
                X23 ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδ9σ/10 (V2 ) ∩ Nδ9σ/10 (V3 ) ∩ B1 ⊂ NCδ9σ/10 (H).
We claim that ∠(X13 , X23 ) ≥ δ 2σ/3 . If this is not the case, then ∠(X13 , X23 ) ≤ δ 2σ/3 ,
and so X13 ∩B1 ⊂ Nδ2σ/3 (X23 ). This together with X23 ∩B1 ⊂ Nδ9σ/10 (V2 )∩B1 implies
that X13 ∩ B1 ⊂ N2δ2σ/3 (V2 ) ∩ B1 . On the other hand, we know that X13 ∩ B1 ⊂
Nδ9σ/10 (V1 ) ∩ B1 , and so X13 ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδ9σ/10 (V1 ) ∩ N2δ2σ/3 (V2 ) ∩ B1 . This in turn
implies X13 ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδ8σ/15 (X12 ) ∩ B1 . Here for “in turn implies”, we are using the
geometric picture that ∠(V1 , V2 ) ≥ δ σ/10 and ∠′ (V1 , V2 ) ≤ δ 9σ/10 mean that for any
τ ≥ δ 9σ/10 (and in particular τ = 2δ 2σ/3 ), we have Nτ (V1 ) ∩ Nτ (V2 ) ∩ B1 is a prism
of dimension τ × τ × (τ /δ σ/10 ) × 1 × · · · × 1 ((n − 3) many 1) whose central plane
is X12 . Therefore, by Remark 3.2, we can conclude that ∠(X13 , X12 ) ≤ δ 8σ/15 , and
so X12 ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδ8σ/15 (X13 ) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/2 (V3 ), contradicting our assumption that
X12 ∩ B1 ̸⊂ Nδσ/2 (V3 ). Thus the claim must hold true.

  As we have said before, Case (ii) of Lemma 8.8 is only an intermediate case and can
be further reduced to Case (i) by slicing arguments. In fact, we have the following
result:
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                            39

Proposition 8.11. There exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ].
Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes. Then there is a ⪆ 1 refinement (F′′ , Y ′′ )
and a function H : U (F′′ , Y ′′ ) → Gr(n, 1) such that for each x ∈ U (F′′ , Y ′′ ), we have

                   dir(F ) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/5 (H(x)⊥ )              for all F ∈ F′′Y ′′ (x).

Proof. We will select η much smaller than some η1 , which is in turn much smaller
than that η1 chosen in the proof of Lemma 8.8. It suffices to argue that Case (ii) of
Lemma 8.8 is still plany up to further suitable refinements. We will argue by using
a slicing argument and exploit multilinear Kakeya for tubes inside each slice. To set
thing up, suppose that we are in Case (ii) in the proof of Lemma 8.8, i.e., there is
a ⪆ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) and a function X : U (F′ , Y ′ ) → Gr(n, n − 3) such that for
each x ∈ U (F′ , Y ′ ), we have

                    X (x) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/2 (dir(F )) for all F ∈ F′Y ′ (x).

  To find a good direction subspace for slicing, let us first sort the points x into

Gr(n, n − 3) with a finitely overlapping union of O(1) small caps of diameter 1/100.
By pigeonholing, there exists (at least) one cap, which we denote by C, such that

  Now we are in the right place to apply the arguments in the proof of [28, Lemma 4.3]
to each (T⃗t , Y⃗t ) in the slice X0⊥ + ⃗t for ⃗t ∈ A. Although the statement of [28,
Lemma 4.3] involves concepts like “η-extremal”, one can see from the proof that we
actually only need the set of tubes to satisfy several resulting properties, which are
much more flexible and easy to check in our setting:
        • (T⃗t , Y⃗t ) has constant multiplicity (8.7), and has union (8.9).
        • The robust transversality assumption for (T⃗t , Y⃗t ) is inherited directly from
          that for (F2 , Y2 ) by recalling (8.2) at the beginning of the proof of Lemma 8.8.
          More precisely, let ε = σ/5, and we want to show that for each y ∈ U (T⃗t , Y⃗t )
          and each line ℓ ∈ Gr(X0⊥ , 1), we have
(8.10)                   |{T ∈ (T⃗t )Y⃗t (y) : ∠(ℓ, dir(T )) ≤ δ 5ε/16 }| ≤ δ −σ+3η1 .

           Suppose this is not true, then there exists some y0 ∈ U (T⃗t , Y⃗t ) and ℓ0 ∈

                   |{T ∈ (T⃗t )Y⃗t (y0 ) : ∠(ℓ0 , dir(T )) ≤ δ 5ε/16 }| > δ −σ+3η1 .

         Let
                   F0 := {F ∈ (F2 )Y2 (y0 ) : ∠(ℓ0 , dir(F ) ∩ X0⊥ ) ≤ δ 5ε/16 },
         then |F0 | > δ −σ+3η1 . Let V0 := X (y0 ) + ℓ0 . Fix any F ∈ F0 . We want to apply
         Lemma 3.15 with

                               V1 = X (y0 ), V1′ = πdir(F ) (X (y0 )),
                                    V2 = ℓ0 , V2′ = dir(F ) ∩ X0⊥ .

         Let us check the assumptions. Since X (y0 )∩B1 ⊂ Nδσ/2 (dir(F )), by Lemma 3.13
         with ρ = δ σ/2 , we get dim V1′ = n − 3 and ∠(V1 , V1′ ) ≤ δ σ/2 ≤ δ 5ε/16 . On the
         other hand, the definition of F0 immediately yields ∠(V2 , V2′ ) ≤ δ 5ε/16 . Fi-
         nally, recall that ∠(X (y0 ), X0 ) ≤ 1/100, and so V1 ∩ B1 ⊂ N1/100 (X0 ) =
         N1/100 ((X0⊥ )⊥ ) ⊂ N1/100 (V2⊥ ). Therefore, we can apply Lemma 3.15 with ρ =
         δ 5ε/16 to conclude that V1 +V2 = V0 and V1′ +V2′ ⊂ dir(F ) are both direct sums,
         which implies V0 = V1 ⊕ V2 ∈ Gr(n, n − 2) and dir(F ) = V1′ ⊕ V2′ by counting
         dimensions. Moreover, ∠(V0 , dir(F )) = ∠(V1 ⊕ V2 , V1′ ⊕ V2′ ) ≤ 3δ 5ε/16 ≤ δ ε/4 .
         This holds for all F ∈ F0 , and so we have

                |{F ∈ (F2 )Y2 (y0 ) : ∠(V0 , dir(F )) ≤ δ ε/4 }| ≥ |F0 | > δ −σ+3η1 ,

        which contradicts (8.2) ((F2 , Y2 ) is a refinement of the flakes and shadings
        therein by trace back the chain of refinements). And so (8.10) must hold true.
      • The version of the multilinear Kakeya inequality due to Carbery and Valdimars-
        son ([4, Theorem 1], see also [28, Theorem 4.2]) does not require any additional
        structural properties of tubes, and so applies equally well to each Tt . (We do
        not even need the tubes Tt to be essentially distinct!)
Therefore, we will omit the remaining details, as they are essentially the same as
those for [28, Lemma 4.3]. As a result of all these processes, we get a ⪆ 1 refinement
(T⃗′t , Y⃗t′ ) of (T⃗t , Y⃗t ) and a function Y⃗t : U (T⃗′t , Y⃗t′ ) → Gr(X0⊥ , 2) for each ⃗t ∈ A, such

(8.11)              dir(T ) ∩ B1 ≤ Nδσ/2 (Y⃗t (y)) for all T ∈ (T⃗′t )Y⃗′ (y).

We point out that the numerology regarding small parameters like ε and η1 only
depends on the properties listed above, and are therefore uniform over all slices. This
yields some admissible requirements on our final choice of η1 .

                                             V1 = X (x), V1′ = πdir(F ) (X (x)),
                                     V2 = YπX0 (x) (x), V2′ = dir(F ) ∩ X0⊥ .
Let us check the assumptions. Since X (x) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/2 (dir(F )), by Lemma 3.13 with
ρ = δ σ/2 , we get dim V1′ = n − 3 and ∠(V1 , V1′ ) ≤ δ σ/2 , i.e., V1′ ∩ B1 ⊂ Nδσ/2 (V1 ). On
