and V1′ + V2′ ⊂ dir(F ) are both direct sums, which implies H⊥ (x) = V1 ⊕ V2 ∈
Gr(n, n − 1) and dir(F ) = V1′ ⊕ V2′ by counting dimensions. Moreover, we have
             dir(F ) ∩ B1 = (V1′ ⊕ V2′ ) ∩ B1 ⊂ N3δσ/2 (V1 ⊕ V2 ) ⊂ Nδσ/5 (H⊥ (x)).
Define H(x) := (H⊥ (x))⊥ ∈ Gr(n, 1). Then H(x)⊥ = H⊥ (x), and we get dir(F )∩B1 ⊂
Nδσ/5 (H(x)⊥ ). This finishes the proof of Proposition 8.11.                    □
Remark 8.12. In general we want the shadings Y ′′ (F ) in the proof to be measurable
sets, but an uncountable union of measurable sets may no longer be measurable. This
issue is purely technical, as we can first apply Lemma 5.4 to the δ-flakes with ρ = δ to
replace the shadings with their δ-neighborhoods, and then do all the slicing arguments
that we have presented. In fact, we can do the replacement at the very beginning
of Section 7 when we define “σ” and “ε-extremal”, i.e., limit the class of shadings
to those that are unions of δ-balls. In this way the distribution of the shadings is
constant at scale δ, which allows us to choose the same (T⃗′t , Y⃗t′ ) and Y⃗t whenever ⃗t
is in a cube of side length δ. This local constancy guarantees the measurability of
Y ′′ (F ). We omit such details that are tedious and not insightful. Another way of
fixing the issue is to use slices with thickness δ and apply a “thickened” version of
     9There could be measurability issues, see Remark 8.12 for some technical explanations.
               THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                                     43

the proof of [28, Lemma 4.3] inside each slice. We will see arguments in this spirit in
Section 9.

  Proposition 8.11 says that extremal sets of δ-flakes must satisfy a weak type of
planiness, which involves a δ σ/5 compression that is ≪ 1 but ≫ δ. However, by
moving to a coarser scale thanks to the stickiness assumption, we can ensure that
a suitable thickening of the flakes agrees with their plane map H in a strong sense.
This is the content of the next lemma. In what follows, for any ρ ∈ [δ, 1] a ρ-cube is
a set of the form [0, ρ)n + x, where x ∈ (ρZ)n .
Lemma 8.13. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all
δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes, and let ρ ∈ [δ 1−ε , δ ε ]. Suppose
there exists a function H : U (F, Y ) → Gr(n, 1) so that for each x ∈ U (F, Y ), we have
                       dir(F ) ∩ B1 ⊂ Nρ (H(x)⊥ )          for all F ∈ FY (x).

H′ are consistent in the sense that there exists x ∈ Q ∩ U (F′ , Y ′ ) with H′ (Q) = H(x).

8.2. Lipschitz regularity of the plane map. So far we have seen the existence of
a plane map, but we still need to know about some of its properties. We will show
that if an extremal set of flakes has a plane map, then after restricting to a refinement,
this plane map must be Lipschitz with controlled Lipschitz norm.
Lemma 8.14. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for
all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes with plane map H, and
let ρ ∈ [δ 1−ε , δ ε ]. Then there exists an ε-extremal refinement (F′ , Y ′ ), so that if
x, y ∈ U (F′ , Y ′ ) are contained in a common ρ-cube, then ∠(H(x), H(y)) ≤ ρ.

     Applying Lemma 8.14 and then deleting a constant fraction of the ρ-cubes to obtain
     √
a     nρ-separated set of cubes, we obtain the following result:

Corollary 8.15. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds
for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes with plane map H,
and let ρ ∈ [δ 1−ε , δ ε ]. Then there exists an ε-extremal refinement (F′ , Y ′ ), so that if
x, y ∈ U (F′ , Y ′ ) satisfy ∥x − y∥ ≤ ρ, then ∠(H(x), H(y)) ≤ ρ.

  Corollary 8.15 says that after restricting to a refinement, the plane map H will be
Lipschitz at a specific scale ρ. Lemma 8.16 iterates this result at many scales.

Lemma 8.16. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for
all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes with plane map H that is
constant on (δ/10n)-cubes. Then there exists an ε-extremal refinement (F′ , Y ′ ), so
that H is δ −ε -Lipschitz on U (F′ , Y ′ ).
                                                                                          √
Proof. After deleting a constant fraction of the (δ/10n)-cubes to obtain a nδ-
separated set of cubes, we can suppose that if x, y ∈ U (F, Y ) satisfy ∥x − y∥ ≤ δ,
then x and y are contained in a common (δ/10n)-cube, and hence H(x) = H(y). Let
N = ⌈2/ε⌉. If η, δ0 are selected sufficiently small, then we can apply Corollary 8.15
iteratively to (F, Y ) for ρ = ρj = δ j/N , j = 1, . . . , N − 1, and the resulting refinement
(F′ , Y ′ ) will be ε-extremal. We claim that H is δ −1/N -Lipschitz on (F′ , Y ′ ). To see
this, let x, y ∈ U (F′ , Y ′ ). If ∥x − y∥ ≤ δ, then H(x) = H(y) and we are done. If
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                              47

∥x − y∥ > δ 1/N , then ∠(H(x), H(y)) ≤ 1 and again we are done. Otherwise, there is
an index 1 ≤ k ≤ N − 1 so that δ (k+1)/N < ∥x − y∥ ≤ δ k/N , and
                         ∠(H(x), H(y)) ≤ δ k/N ≤ δ −1/N ∥x − y∥ .
This verifies the claim, and thus the conclusion of the lemma.                                □

  Combining the results we obtained so far with Lemma 7.4, we have the following
conclusion:

Lemma 8.17. For all ε, δ0 > 0, there exist δ ∈ (0, δ0 ] and an ε-extremal set of
δ-flakes that has a δ −ε -Lipschitz plane map.

Proof. By Lemma 7.4, we can take an η-extremal set of δ-flakes for a very small η.
Then Proposition 8.11 gives the weak planiness, which is further upgraded to a strong
one by Lemma 8.13 with ρ = δ σ/5 . Finally, Lemma 8.16 provides the desired Lipschitz
property of the plane map.                                                         □

8.3. Local grains. With Lemma 8.17 in hand, we are finally in the position to study
the graininess structure. We will show that if an extremal set of flakes has a Lipschitz
plane map, then after restricting to a refinement, the flakes arrange themselves into
parallel slab segments, which are called local grains. In what follows, H will be a
plane map for a set of flakes.
   Our goal is to show that the set of local grains inside a cube arrange themselves
into an AD-regular set, in the sense of Definition 8.3. As a first step, Lemma 8.18
controls how many local grains can occur inside a cube at a given scale. The idea
of the proof is similar to that of [28, Lemma 4.9], except that we use a projection
method to handle local hairbrushes, instead of directly executing a Córdoba-type L2
argument. The reason is that the geometry of flakes is much more complicated than
tubes and calls for a more robust argument. Also, we write things in a more detailed
and rigorous way.

Lemma 8.18. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all
δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes with δ −η -Lipschitz plane map
H. Let ρ ∈ [δ 1−ε , δ ε ] and let τ ∈ [ρ1−ε , ρ1/2 ]. For each τ -cube Q̃ intersecting U (F, Y ),
define H(Q̃) := H(yQ̃ ), where yQ̃ is any fixed point in Q̃ ∩ U (F, Y ).
   Then there exists an ⪆ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) which is 2η-extremal, so that for each
τ -cube Q̃ intersecting U (F, Y ), we have

to ε.

   Lemma 8.18 says that when restricted to a τ -cube and examined at scale ρ, the set
U (F′ , Y ′ ) looks like a union of about (τ /ρ)1−σ parallel grains, whose normal directions
all coincide and are given by the plane map H. Lemma 8.19 asserts that a similar
statement continues to hold if we replace a τ -cube with a (potentially much larger)
τ × ρ1/2 × ρ1/2⃗1 thick slab segment, whose central hyperplane is orthogonal to the
plane map H. To prove this lemma, we need to show that at scale ρ, the grains inside
a τ -cube are pieces of the grains inside a ρ1/2 -cube. The proof is similar to that in
[28, Lemma 4.10], but written in a fully rigorous way.

Lemma 8.19. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for

map H. Let ρ ∈ [δ 1−ε , δ ε ] and let τ ∈ [ρ1−ε , ρ1/2 ]. For each ρ1/2 -cube Q̄ intersecting
U (F, Y ), define H(Q̄) := H(yQ̄ ), where yQ̄ is any fixed point in Q̄ ∩ U (F, Y ).

is ε-extremal. And so the proof is completed.                                                    □

   To prove Proposition 8.4, the remaining issues are to upgrade Lemma 8.19 to a
version that handles multiple scales ρ and τ simultaneously. For this purpose, there
is nothing special about the geometry of flakes, and the iteration steps are exactly
the same as those in [28, Lemma 4.11 and 4.12]. To avoid unnecessary repetitions,
we only state the corresponding results for flakes.
   Firstly, for a fixed ρ, Lemma 8.20 records what happens when we iterate Lemma 8.19
for many different scales τ .
Lemma 8.20. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all
δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes with δ −η -Lipschitz plane map H.
Let ρ ∈ [δ 1−ε , δ ε ]. For each ρ1/2 -cube Q̄ intersecting U (F, Y ), define H(Q̄) := H(yQ̄ ),
where yQ̄ is any fixed point in Q̄ ∩ U (F, Y ).
  Then there exists an ε-extremal refinement (F′ , Y ′ ), so that for each ρ1/2 -cube Q̄
intersecting U (F, Y ) and each line segment J ⊂ H(Q̄) of length τ ≥ ρ, we have
                                                        
                      Eρ J ∩ πH(Q̄) (U (F′ , Y ′ ) ∩ Q̄) ≤ ρ−ε (τ /ρ)1−σ .

   Next, Lemma 8.21 records what happens when we iterate Lemma 8.20 for many
different scales ρ.
Lemma 8.21. For all ε > 0, there exist η, δ0 > 0 so that the following holds for all
δ ∈ (0, δ0 ]. Let (F, Y ) be an η-extremal set of δ-flakes with δ −η -Lipschitz plane map
H.
              THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                         57

  Then there exists an ε-extremal refinement (F′ , Y ′ ), so that for all x ∈ U (F, Y ), all
ρ ≥ δ, and all line segments J ⊂ H(x) of length τ ≥ ρ, we have
                                                            
                  Eρ J ∩ πH(x) (U (F′ , Y ′ ) ∩ B(x, ρ1/2 )) ≤ δ −ε (τ /ρ)1−σ .

In particular, πH(x) (U (F′ , Y ′ ) ∩ B(x, ρ1/2 )) is a (ρ, 1 − σ, δ −ε )1 -ADset.

  We are now ready to prove Proposition 8.4.

Proof of Proposition 8.4. Combining Lemma 8.17 with Lemma 8.21 yields a set of
δ-flakes (F, Y ) satisfying all the requirements, except that the plane map H for it
is only δ −η -Lipschitz (for some 0 < η ≪ ε). This can be fixed via a mild rescaling
similar to [28, Lemma 3.5]. The arguments are straightforward (though somewhat
tedious), and so are omitted.                                                     □


                           9. An unusual slicing argument

   Recall that for the proof of Theorem 6.5, we start by assuming σ > 0 and aim
for some contradiction. So far we have established the planiness and the local grains
structure. To continue the proof, we will use certain slicing arguments, and here is
an outline of the key steps:
     (i) Choose a suitable X ∈ Gr(n, n − 3), and cut the δ-flakes up by (δ, 3)-flakes
         orthogonal to X in Rn . We get a bunch of δ-tubes in each slice ((δ, 3)-flake).
    (ii) Use a projection method and (n−3)-plane Kakeya in Rn−1 (induction hypoth-
         esis: Theorem 1.1 when n is replaced with n − 1) to show that the δ-tubes in
         each slice is essentially distinct.
   (iii) Apply the factoring lemma (Lemma 4.5) in each slice to get a bunch of convex
         sets of dimensions δ⃗1 × a × b × 1 (δ ≤ a ≤ b ≤ 1) that factors the δ-tubes.
         The convex sets themselves are Katz–Tao, while the δ-tubes in each convex
         set is Frostman.
   (iv) If b ≈ δ, then the δ-tubes in each slice are Katz–Tao, and so are essentially
         disjoint by R3 Kakeya for tubes (Theorem 1.2). As a result, the original δ-
         flakes are essentially disjoint, contradicting the extremal property. Therefore,
         we may assume that b ≫ δ.
    (v) By R3 Kakeya for planks ([14, Lemma 6.4], a by-product of R3 Kakeya for
         tubes), in each slice, the union of δ-tubes must fill out all the convex sets from
         Step (iii). This can be viewed as an initial step that grows grains.
   (vi) If a ≫ δ, then by combining the growth in all slices, each δ-flake must fill
         out a much thicker a-flake containing it, which allows us to go efficiently from
         scale δ to scale a (by self-similarity) and gain an exponent, contradicting the
         extremal property.
  (vii) Otherwise, we are in the case when a ≈ δ and b ≫ δ. We know that in each
         slice, the δ-tubes have grown into much wider δ⃗1 × b × 1 grains, which we call
58            CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

        medium grains 11. However, since the direction of such anisotropic growth in
        Rn may not be uniform over all slices, we cannot directly grow each δ-flake
        into a much wider δ × b × ⃗1 plank. To overcome this difficulty, we need to
        play with both medium grains and local grains, the compatibility of which
        provides us with some additional rigidity. More precisely, the medium grains
        can be used to regularize the orientations of local grains, i.e., the plane map
        is kind of concentrated when we walk along each medium grain.
 (viii) The strategy in Step (vii) is good but still not enough, as we need to regularize
        the behaviour for different medium grains. Fortunately, by choosing (n − 2)
        “transversal” X ∈ Gr(n, n − 3) and applying all the previous discussions with
        respect to each of them, we can make sure that the plane map is kind of
        concentrated over each δ-flake.
  (ix) By the concentration property of the plane map obtained in Step (viii), we
        can combine the local grains along each δ-flake to grow it into a much wider
        δ × b′ × ⃗1 plank for some b′ ≫ δ (although b′ may be much smaller than b).
   (x) By self-similarity, the δ-flakes in each b′ -flake (from the b′ -cover) are Frostman,
        and so the corresponding δ × b′ × ⃗1 planks are also Frostman, thus filling
        out the b′ -flake via a local Córdoba-type L2 argument. This allows us to go
        efficiently from scale δ to scale b′ (still by self-similarity) and gain an exponent,
        contradicting the extremal property. The whole proof is completed.
  In a nutshell, we use the known R3 Kakeya in many scenarios (projection when
n = 4, slicing, growing medium grains), and by combining the grains structure with
the slicing method in an organic way, we are able to prove the (n − 2)-plane sticky
Kakeya theorem without resorting to advanced projection theory and additive com-
binatorics. More precisely, these ingredients (that distinguish R and C) are implicitly
used whenever we apply R3 Kakeya, but we need nothing more for (n − 2)-plane
Kakeya in Rn than for line Kakeya in R3 .
   The above outline involves many informal reductions, e.g., often we can only expect
a property to hold for “most” objects but not “all” of them. And our main task will
be to flesh out the outline in a rigorous way by the language of shadings. We will
finish Steps (i)–(vi) in this section, and leave Steps (vii)–(x) to the next section.
The planiness and the local grains structure are only used in the last four steps
(Section 10), while the self-similarity property is only used in Step (vi) and (x).
   We now begin this long proof. It is important to keep in mind that (F, Y ) is fixed
and is η-extremal. η will be reserved as the extremal parameter throughout the proof
and it will be chosen to be sufficiently small later on. First of all, by Proposition 8.4,
for any η, δ0 > 0, there exist δ ∈ (0, δ0 ] and an η-extremal set of δ-flakes (F, Y ), such
that for all ρ ∈ [δ, 1] and x ∈ U (F, Y ), πH(x) (U (F, Y ) ∩ B(x, ρ1/2 )) is a (ρ, 1 − σ, δ −η )1 -
ADset. Here H is a 1-Lipschitz plane map for (F, Y ).

further assume that for some constant λ, we have
                                            |Y ω,1 (T )|/|T | ∼ λ ⪆ δ η
for all ω ∈ Λ1 and all T ∈ Tω,1 . It is easy to verify that λ and (F1 , Y1 ) satisfy all the
desired properties.                                                                       □

  For 0 < η ≪ ε1 , let λ and (F1 , Y1 ) be the output of Proposition 9.2, and let
{(Tω,1 , Y ω,1 )}ω∈Λ1 be defined as in (9.7) and (9.8).

Remark 9.7. [14, Lemma 6.4] initially gives Lemma 9.6 in the range 0 < a ≤ b ≤ b∗
for suitable η∗ , b∗ > 0 depending on ε. However, only a needs to be sufficiently small.
The point is that when b is bounded below, an a × b × 1 plank has only one thin
direction. This regime is controlled by the standard Córdoba-type L2 estimates for
slabs, which estimate overlap using pairwise slab intersections (see Lemma 13.9).
                                 n         o
  Indeed, choose η ≤ min η∗ , 2Cε 3 , where C3 is the constant in Lemma 13.9. If
b ≤ b∗ , then [14, Lemma 6.4] applies directly. If b > b∗ , choose a0 sufficiently small
so that aη0 ≤ b∗ . After decreasing a0 further, the Frostman hypothesis also gives
CF (L, B1 ) ≤ a−η for a ≤ a0 . We then have aη ≤ b, so Lemma 13.9, with (δ, b1 , b2 ) =
(a, b, 1) gives |U (L, Y )| ≳ aC3 η ≥ aε/2 . We can decrease a0 to absorb the implicit
constant and get |U (L, Y )| ≥ aε .
  In the application immediately below, the rescaled planks have dimensions (δ/b) ×
(δ/a) × 1. Thus the parameter called a in the preceding lemma corresponds to δ/b
here, while its parameter b corresponds to δ/a. Since δ ≤ a ≤ b, we have δb ≤ aδ ≤ 1.
Moreover, b ≥ δ 1−η1 implies δ/b ≤ δ η1 ≤ a0 for sufficiently small δ. This verifies the
required smallness of the shortest rescaled side.

                   10. Medium grains and the end of the proof

   In this section, we will continue the proof in Section 9 and finish Steps (vii)–(x)
therein. Recall from Step (v) that each T ∈ Tω,3 grows into a δ⃗1 × a × b × 1 grain
W ∈ Wω (with T ⊂ W ), and by Step (iv) and Step (vi), we may assume that b ≥ δ 1−η1
and a ≤ δ 1−η2 . Now we choose η2 ≪ η1 . This essentially means that a ≈ δ and b ≫ δ,
and the grains roughly have dimensions δ⃗1 × b × 1. From now on, we will call them
medium grains, which extends the δ-tube T in some normal direction of a δ-flake
F containing T . As outlined at the beginning of Section 9, we need to analyze the
compatibility of medium grains and local grains to regularize the plane map, which
is vital for further grains-growing procedures. Now we turn to the details.
  Step (vii). Let us first show that the plane map H cannot change too much along
the tubes corresponding to X1 (up to a refinement). More explicitly, we will prove
the following result:

Proposition 10.1. Suppose 0 < η ≪ ε1 ≪ η2 ≪ η1 ≪ σ, then there exists δ0 > 0 so
that the following holds for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ].
   For each ω ∈ Γ and T ∈ Tω,3 , there exists some HT ∈ Gr(n, 1) depending only on
T , such that ∠(H(x), HT ) ≲ δ η1 /2 for all x ∈ Y ω,3 (T ).


Proof. Fix any ω ∈ Γ and T ∈ Tω,3 . Then there exists a W ∈ Wω with T ⊂ W ,
and an F ∈ F1 with T ∈ T(F ). Let s := min{b, δ 1/2 }, then s ≥ δ 1−η1 ≥ a (as
η2 ≤ η1 ≤ 1/2). By (9.16) with s in place of r, for each x ∈ Y ω,3 (T ), we have

                   |U (Tω,2 , Y ω,1 ) ∩ W ∩ B(x, s)| ⪆ δ ε1 +2η |W ∩ B(x, s)|,

where W ∩ B(x, s) is a rectangular box in Πω of size δ⃗1 × a × s × s.
   Let PW,x ⊂ B(x, s) be the a⃗1 × s × s plate segment obtained by thickening the
sides of W ∩ B(x, s) along X1 from δ to a. We may assume that PW,x is centered
at x. Since s ≫ a, by a rescaled version of Definition 3.3, it makes sense to talk
about its direction subspace dir(PW,x ), which is well defined up to error a/s by (a
rescaled version of) Lemma 3.6. We may assume that dir(T ) ⊂ dir(PW,x ) ⊂ X1⊥ .
Also, since each PW,x is parallel to W , we can make sure that dir(PW,x ) is the same
for all x ∈ Y ω,3 (T ). In particular, each PW,x can be tiled by ∼ s/a parallel a⃗1 × a × s
tube segments pointing in dir(T ) = dir(F ) ∩ X1⊥ , as well as in some ℓW,x ∈ Gr(n, 1)
perpendicular to dir(T ), where ℓW,x are the same for all x ∈ Y ω,3 (T ). Let ℓW be the

Remark 10.5. Intuitively, for each F ∈ F, the shading Y (n−2) (F ) is simultaneously
well-behaved under (n − 2) transverse (to be checked!) tilings of δ-tubes, in the sense
                                                                    ω,(k)
that H(x) deviates by ≲ δ η1 /2 when we walk along T ∈ T(k) (F )∩T∗       (1 ≤ k ≤ n−2),

as long as x remains confined to the set Y            (F ). One may suspect that this
already guarantees the existence of some HF ∈ Gr(n, 1), so that ∠(H(x), HF ) ≲ δ η1/2
for all x ∈ Y (n−2) (F ), as any two points x, y ∈ Y (n−2) (F ) can reach each other by
walking along a broken line aligned with the tilings (like a contagious structure).
However, there is a caveat that the corner points of this broken line may not lie
entirely in Y (n−2) (F ). To settle this problem, we should employ certain backward
iteration to construct another suitable refinement Y3 (F ) ⊂ Y (1) (F ), which may not
have any immediate inclusion relation with Y (n−2) (F ). This is why we still need
Step (B) and (C).

     For 0 < η ≪ η0 ≪ η1 ≪ σ, let (F, Y3 ) be the output of Proposition 10.4.
     This finishes Step (viii).
  From now on, we can forget about the slicing structures, because Steps (ix) and (x)
depend only on the concentration property of H we get in this step, which is preserved
under any refinement of (F, Y3 ).
 Step (ix). Now we are ready to grow the flakes into wider planks (up to a refine-
ment). More precisely, we will prove the following result:

Proposition 10.7. Suppose 0 < η ≪ ε3 ≪ η1 ≪ σ, then there exists δ0 > 0 so that
the following holds for all δ ∈ (0, δ0 ].
  There exists F3 ⊂ F with |F3 | ≥ δ ε3 |F|, such that each F ∈ F3 is contained in a
plank LF of dimensions δ × δ 1−η1 /2 × ⃗1 with |U (F, Y ) ∩ LF | ⪆ δ 5ε3 |LF |.

regular.

   If we define a shading ȲCb′ on (F4 )Cb′ by ȲCb′ (FCb′ ) := U (L(FCb′ ), YL ) ∩ FCb′ for
each FCb′ ∈ (F4 )Cb′ , then λ((F4 )Cb′ , ȲCb′ ) ⪆ (Cb′ )(24ε3 +18η)/η1 . This together with the
first bullet point means that ((F4 )Cb′ , ȲCb′ ) satisfies Items (a) and (b) in Definition 7.1
with δ = Cb′ and ε = (24ε3 + 18η)/η1 . By Remark 7.3, if we choose max{η, ε3 } ≪
min{η1 , η2 }, then
         |U ((F4 )Cb′ , ȲCb′ )| ≥ (Cb′ )σ+η2 ≥ δ (1−η1 /2)(σ+η2 ) = δ σ−(η1 σ/2+η1 η2 /2−η2 ) .
By choosing η2 ≤ η1 σ/4, the lower bound becomes |U ((F4 )Cb′ , ȲCb′ )| ≥ δ σ−η1 σ/4 . On
the other hand, since U ((F4 )Cb′ , ȲCb′ ) ⊂ U (F, Y ) and (F, Y ) is η-extremal, we have
the upper bound |U (F, Y )| ≤ δ σ−η . By choosing η ≤ η1 σ/8 and then letting δ0 be
small enough, we obtain a contradiction between the upper and lower bounds. This
finishes Step (x).
  In summary, under the initial assumption that σ > 0, we always get a contradiction
by carrying out all Steps (i)–(x). In other words, we must have σ = 0 at the very
beginning. This completes the proof of Theorem 6.5.

                                11. Proof of Theorem 6.2

  In this section, we will first deduce Part (B) of Theorem 6.2 from Part (A), and
then prove Part (A) by using Theorem 6.5.

11.1. Uniformity. Before everything, let us talk more about uniformity. In Sec-
tion 2.4, we have seen that any set of δ-flakes has a uniform refinement by multiscale
pigeonholing. If we modify the pigeonholing procedure so that at each step we pi-
geonhole not only |F[Fρ ]| but also ∆max (F[Fρ ]), then we can also make ∆max (F[Fρ ])
constant up to a factor ∼ 1. In particular, this implies that CF (F[Fρ ]) is constant up
to a factor ∼ 1. In fact, we can do something more:
88            CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

Proposition 11.1. Every set F of essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn has a subset
F′ of cardinality |F′ | ⪆ |F| so that for any two scales δ ≤ τ ≤ θ ≤ 1, we have |F′τ [Fθ′ ]|,
                                                                                        −1
CF (F′τ [Fθ′ ]), and ∆max (F′τ [Fθ′ ]) all constant up to a factor δ −C(n)(log log(1/δ)) ≈ 1 as
Fθ′ varies in F′θ .
                                                                                           2
Proof. Let M and ρk be as in Definition 2.1, then since (log(1/δ))CM ⪅ 1, by pi-
geonholing Fρj [Fρk ] for all integer pairs {(k, j) : 0 ≤ k ≤ j ≤ M } in lexicographically
descending order, we immediately get a ⪆ 1 refinement F′ that satisfies the conclu-
sions at least for all τ = ρj and θ = ρk with j ≥ k. Now we check the conclusions for
general τ and θ. For this purpose, let τ ∈ [ρj , ρj−1 ] and θ ∈ [ρk , ρk−1 ] (1 ≤ j, k ≤ M ).
Fix any Fρ′ j ⊂ Fτ′ ⊂ Fρ′ j−1 and Fρ′ k ⊂ Fθ′ ⊂ Fρ′ k−1 . Note that

               |F′ρj−1 [Fρ′ k ]| ≤ |F′τ [Fθ′ ]| ≤ |F′ρj [Fρ′ k−1 ]| ⪅ |F′ρj−1 [Fρ′ k ]|
        ∆max (F′ρj−1 [Fρ′ k ]) ≤ ∆max (F′τ [Fθ′ ]) ≤ ∆max (F′ρj [Fρ′ k−1 ]) ⪅ ∆max (F′ρj−1 [Fρ′ k ]).

Finally, by combining these with Definition 4.2, we immediately get CF (F′τ [Fθ′ ]) ≈
                                                                                       −1
CF (F′ρj−1 [Fρ′ k ]). And one can easily check that all factors are bounded by δ −C(n)M .
So the proof is completed.                                                              □

   Therefore, we can modify Definition 2.1 (and thus Definition 2.2) in the following
sense: Whenever we say F is uniform (at some scales), we additionally assume multi-
scale uniformity on convex Wolff axiom constants (between these scales). In view of
Proposition 11.1, this convention is entirely harmless in practice. It also allows us to
write expressions such as |Fτ [Fθ ]|, ∆max (Fτ [Fθ ]), CF (Fτ [Fθ ]) without ambiguity (up
to a ≈ 1 factor), as long as F is uniform. And in this case, we will often omit words
like “for all Fθ ∈ Fθ ” for simplicity.
     An important fact is that the stickiness property is stable under uniformization:

Lemma 11.2. Let η > 0, and let (F, Y ) be a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes in
B1 ⊂ Rn , with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
     (A) If F is δ −η Frostman at every scale, then there exist λ ⪆ δ η and a uniform
         subset F′ ⊂ F, such that λ(F′ , Y ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ), |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all F ∈ F′ ,
         and F′ is ⪅ δ −2η Frostman at every scale.
     (B) If F is δ −η Katz–Tao at every scale, then there exist λ ⪆ δ η and a uniform
         subset F′ ⊂ F, such that λ(F′ , Y ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ), |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all F ∈ F′ ,
         and F′ is δ −η Katz–Tao at every scale.

Proof. The construction of F′ is the same for both parts. Let (F′′ , Y ) be a ⪆ 1
refinement of (F, Y ) so that there is λ > 0 such that |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all F ∈ F′′ .
Then |F′′ | ⪆ δ η |F| and λ ∼ λ(F′′ , Y ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . By Proposition 11.1, there is a
subset F′ ⊂ F′′ with |F′ | ⪆ |F′′ | so that F′ is uniform. Note that (F′ , Y ) is still a ⪆ 1
refinement of (F, Y ), and |F′ | ⪆ δ η |F|, λ(F′ , Y ) ∼ λ(F′′ , Y ) ⪆ δ η . It remains to check
the required stickiness property for each part.
            THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                                     89

   (A) For each δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, by pigeonholing, there is an Fρ such that |F′ [Fρ ]| ⪆
       δ η |F[Fρ ]|, which means CF (F′ [Fρ ]) ⪅ δ −η CF (F[Fρ ]) ≤ δ −2η . Then by unifor-
       mity of F′ , this holds for all Fρ ∈ F′ρ . This proves Part (A).
   (B) For each δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, since F′ρ ⊂ Fρ , we have ∆max (F′ρ ) ≤ ∆max (Fρ ) ≤ δ −η .
       This proves Part (B).
So the proof is completed.                                                                          □

Proof of Part (B) of Theorem 6.2 assuming Part (A). Fix ε > 0 and let η1 > 0 be
the η obtained from Theorem 6.2(A). Decreasing η1 if necessary, we can assume that
η1 < ε/3, and 2(2n − 1)/η1 = M is an integer. Let η = 1/M 2 . Let (F, Y ) be a set of
essentially distinct δ-flakes that is δ −η Katz–Tao at every scale and has λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
It suffices to prove that |U (F, Y )| ≥ δ 2ε |F ||F|.
  By Lemma 11.2(B), there exist λ ⪆ δ η and a uniform subset F′ ⊂ F, such that
λ(F′ , Y ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ), |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all F ∈ F′ , and F′ is δ −η Katz–Tao at every
scale. Note that |F′ | ⪆ δ η |F|.
    Consider the sequence of scales ρm = δ m/M , m = M, . . . , 0. We have ρm /ρm−1 =
δ 1/M ≥ δ η1 , and

        ∆max (F′ρm [Fρm−1 ]) ≤ ∆max (F′ρm ) ≤ δ −η = (ρm−1 /ρm )ηM ≤ (ρm−1 /ρm )η1 .

Now we can apply Lemma 11.5 to F′ . We obtain a set of J translations {Rj }Jj=1 , with
J ⪅M δ −1/M (|F ||F′ |)−1 (thus J ≤ δ −η1 (|F ||F′ |)−1 ), so that the set F′′ = Jj=1 Rj (F′ )
                                                                                      F

is ⪅ 1 essentially distinct and ⪅M δ −(2n−1)/M (thus δ −2η1 /3 ) Frostman at every scale.
Define the shading Y ′ on F′′ in the obvious way: For any F ∈ F′ and 1 ≤ j ≤ J,
let Y ′ (Rj (F )) := Rj (Y (F )). Then λ(F′′ , Y ′ ) = λ(F′ , Y ) ⪆ δ η . Intuitively we want to
apply Theorem 6.2(A) to (F′′ , Y ′ ), but since the flakes may not be genuinely essentially
distinct, we need some technical steps.
   Since F′′ is ⪅ 1 essentially distinct, there is F′′′ ⊂ F′′ with |F′′′ | ⪆ |F′′ | so that F′′′
is essentially distinct. By our construction of F′′ , we have |Y ′ (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all
F ∈ F′′ , so (F′′′ , Y ′ ) is a ⪆ 1 refinement of (F′′ , Y ′ ), and λ(F′′′ , Y ′ ) ∼ λ(F′′ , Y ′ ) ⪆ δ η .
For each δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, by pigeonholing, there is an Fρ such that |F′′′ [Fρ ]| ⪆ |F′′ [Fρ ]|,
which means CF (F′′′ [Fρ ]) ⪅ CF (F′′ [Fρ ]) ≤ δ −2η1 /3 . Then by uniformity of F′′′ , this
holds for all Fρ ∈ F′′′                               ′′′

  Now (F′′′ , Y ′ ) satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem 6.2(A) with η1 in place of η.

11.3. Theorem 6.5 implies Theorem 6.2(A). For this purpose, we need the fol-
lowing analogue of [13, Lemma 7.2]:

Lemma 11.7. Let ε, δ > 0 and let F be a uniform set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn . Suppose

  The proof of Lemma 11.7 is similar to that of [13, Lemma 7.2], but we still present
the details for completeness. The key ingredient is the following probability lemma,
which should be compared with Lemma 12.5.
Lemma 11.8. Let F be a set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn . Then it contains a subset F′
with CF (F′ ) ⪅ CF (F) and |F′ | ≈ δ −2 CF (F)−1 .

   We will prove Lemma 11.8 in Appendix A, where we collect a couple of related
probabilistic arguments. Now we are ready to prove Lemma 11.7. The proof consists
of using Lemma 11.8 at many scales.

  For each m = 1, . . . , M , we have
                     (ρm /ρm−1 )−2+ε ⪅ |F′ρm [Fρm−1 ]| ⪅ (ρm /ρm−1 )−2 .
This defines F′ , and implies that for every ρm ,
                         (δ/ρm )−2+ε ⪅M |F′ [Fρm ]| ⪅M (δ/ρm )−2 .
Then for any δ ≤ ρ ≤ 1, there exists ρm ≤ ρ ≤ ρm−1 (recall ρm ⪆ δ ε ρm−1 ), and so
             2                    2
        δ 3ε−ε (δ/ρ)−2 ≤ δ 2ε−ε (δ/ρ)−2+ε ⪅ (δ/ρm )−2+ε ⪅M |F′ [Fρm ]| ≤ |F′ [Fρ ]|;
                  |F′ [Fρ ]| ≤ |F′ [Fρm−1 ]| ⪅M (δ/ρm−1 )−2 ⪅ δ −2ε (δ/ρ)−2 .
On the other hand, for each m = 1, . . . , M , we have
                   CF (F′ρm [Fρm−1 ]) ⪅ CF (Fρm [Fρm−1 ]) ⪅ (ρm /ρm−1 )−ε .
Given our bound for F′ρm [Fρm−1 ], this implies
                            ∆max (F′ρm [Fρm−1 ]) ⪅ (ρm /ρm−1 )−ε .
By the submultiplicative property of ∆max in Lemma 11.3 (and also Remark 11.4),
we get
                                       ∆max (F′ ) ⪅M δ −ε .
94           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

Therefore, we can conclude that F′ which is δ −3ε -sticky.                                   □

Proof of Theorem 6.2(A) assuming Theorem 6.5. Fix ε > 0 and let η1 > 0 be the η
obtained from Theorem 6.5. Decreasing η1 if necessary, we can suppose that 6/η1 = M
is an integer. Let η = 1/M 2 . Let (F, Y ) be a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes
that is δ −η Frostman at every scale and has λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . It suffices to prove that
|U (F, Y )| ≥ δ ε .
  By Lemma 11.2(A), there exist λ ⪆ δ η and a uniform subset F′ ⊂ F, such that
λ(F′ , Y ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ), |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all F ∈ F′ , and F′ is ⪅ δ −2η Frostman at
every scale.
    Consider the sequence of scales ρm = δ m/M , m = M, . . . , 0. We have ρm /ρm−1 =
δ 1/M ≥ δ η1 /3 , and uniformity of F′ implies
       CF (F′ρm [Fρm−1 ]) ⪅ CF (F′ [Fρm−1 ]) ⪅ δ −2η = (ρm−1 /ρm )2ηM = (ρm−1 /ρm )η1 /3 .
Now we can apply Lemma 11.7 to F′ , and obtain a subset F′′ which is δ −η1 -sticky.
Since |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ for all F ∈ F′′ ⊂ F′ , we know that λ(F′′ , Y ) ∼ λ ⪆ δ η (thus
λ(F′′ , Y ) ≥ δ η1 ). Now by applying Theorem 6.5 to (F′′ , Y ), we get
                                |U (F, Y )| ≥ |U (F′′ , Y )| ≥ δ ε ,
as desired. This proves Theorem 6.2(A).                                                      □

     Therefore, Theorem 6.2 holds true.

                     12. General cases and two main lemmas

  Having solved the sticky case (Theorem 6.2), we now turn to the reduction of the
general case. The idea follows tightly with the paper by Guth, Wang and Zahl [14].
We will establish the statements KKT (β) and KF (β) and prove two self-improving
lemmas that will establish Theorem 1.1.
  In this section, we first introduce and analyze Kakeya-type estimates for δ-flakes
satisfying Katz–Tao and Frostman convex Wolff axioms. Then we will set up the
general induction framework of proving Theorem 1.1.
Definition 12.1. We write KKT (β) for the following statement. For any ε > 0, there
exists η = η(ε, β) so that the following holds for any 0 < δ ≤ δ(ε, β). Let F be a set
of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn with the following properties.
       • ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η .
       • Y is a shading on F with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
Then
                                      µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε |F|β .

  Note that KKT (1) is trivial. Our goal is to prove KKT (0). When |F| ∼ δ −2 , KKT (β)
gives µ(F) ≲ δ −2β−ε , which is equivalent to |U (F)| ≳ δ 2β+ε .
              THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                    95

Definition 12.2. We write KF (β) for the following statement. For any ε > 0, there
exists η = η(ε, β) so that the following holds for any 0 < δ ≤ δ(ε, β). Let F be a set
of essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn with the following properties.
       • CF (F, B1 ) ≤ δ −η .
       • Y is a shading on F with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
Then
                             µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε δ −2β (|F||F |)1−β/n .
Or equivalently,
                              |U (F, Y )| ≥ δ ε δ 2β (|F||F |)β/n .

   The above two definitions depend on the dimension n, which is nevertheless often
clear from the context. So for clarity, we will not specify the dependence on n in
KKT (β) and KF (β).
   Note that CF (F, B1 ) ≤ δ −η implies |F| ≥ δ −2+η , and is in fact equivalent to
∆max (F) ≲ δ −η when |F| ∼ δ −2 . In this case, both KKT (β) and KF (β) imply
|U (F)| ≳ δ 2β+ε . However, when |F| ≫ δ −2 (and β > 0), we have (|F||F |)β/n ≫ 1,
so KF (β) gives a stronger bound. The exact exponent β/n in this final factor is not
crucial in our analysis, and the argument would work equally well with any positive
exponent cβ, 0 < c < 1/(n − 2). There is one step (see Remark 15.3) where it is
important that c < 1/(n − 2), which differs from the range in [14], and we will explain
it at an appropriate time.
Proposition 12.3. If KKT (β) holds, then we get for free the following slightly more
general version of KKT (β): For any ε > 0, there exists η = η(ε, β) so that the
following holds for any 0 < δ ≤ δ(ε, β). Suppose F is a set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn ,
τ ≤ δ, and
       • ∆max (F) ≤ τ −η .
       • Y is a shading on F with λ(F, Y ) ≥ τ η .
Then
                                    µ(F, Y ) ≤ τ −ε |F|β .
Similarly, KF (β) implies a slightly more general version of KF (β).
Proof. Exactly the same as that of [14, Remark 3.6], and so omitted.                 □
  These slightly more general versions will be useful when we replace a set of δ-flakes
(F, Y ) with a thickening (Fρ , Yρ ), for which we only know that λ(Fρ , Yρ ) ≥ δ η .
  For β ∈ [0, 1], the two main lemmas in the whole proof are
Main Lemma 1. KKT (β) implies KF (β).
Main Lemma 2. If β > 0 and KKT (β) and KF (β) hold, then KKT (β − ν) holds for
ν = ν(β) > 0. Moreover ν(β) is monotone in β.
96         CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

 Since the bound KKT (1) is trivial, the two main lemmas imply that KKT (β) and
KF (β) hold for all β > 0, which implies Theorem 1.1.
  Before we proceed with the proofs of these two main lemmas, let us first give a
brief roadmap:
     • Firstly, the proofs require some flexibility in applications of the two statements
       KKT (β) and KF (β): In Section 12.1, we will extend their usage to sets of flakes
       with larger convex Wolff constants; while in Section 13, we will further extend
       their usage to planks instead of just flakes, as well as to flakes that are factored
       by planks.
     • Next, in Section 14, we will establish a multiscale decomposition (Lemma 14.5)
       that roughly provides a useful dichotomy between sticky and non-sticky cases.
     • Then, in Section 15, we prove Main Lemma 1 by using the “Frostman”
       part of sticky Kakeya (Theorem 6.2(A)) and the multiscale decomposition
       (Lemma 14.5(A)).
     • Finally, in Section 16, we prove Main Lemma 2 by first reducing it to a “very
       not sticky” case (Lemma 16.1) using the “Katz–Tao” part of sticky Kakeya
       (Theorem 6.2(B)) and the multiscale decomposition (Lemma 14.5(B)), and
       then prove Lemma 16.1 by using a carefully designed two-scale induction
       scheme.

12.1. Multiplicity bounds for larger values of ∆max (F) or CF (F). The next two
results say that KKT (β) and KF (β) imply multiplicity bounds for families of flakes
with any value of ∆max (F) or CF (F).

Lemma 12.4. Suppose that KKT (β) holds. Then for any ε > 0, there exists η =
η(ε, β) > 0 such that the following holds for any 0 < δ < δ(ε, β). Suppose that (F, Y )
is a set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . Then
                            µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε ∆max (F)1−β |F|β .

Proof. Exactly the same as that for [14, Lemma 3.7], and so omitted.                     □

Lemma 12.5. Suppose that F is a set of (essentially distinct) δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn .
Let R1 , . . . , RJ be independent random rigid motions of size ≤ 1, with J = ⌈CF (F)⌉,
and let F′ = Jj=1 Rj (F). Then with high probability the flakes of F′ are essentially
                  F

distinct up to multiplicity ⪅ 1 and also CF (F′ ) ⪅ 1. After refining F′ to make the
flakes essentially distinct, we have |F′ | ≈ CF (F)|F| and CF (F′ ) ⪅ 1.

  Here a random rigid motion of size ≤ 1 is a random rotation composed with a
random translation of size ≤ 1. We will prove Lemma 12.5 in Appendix A, where we
collect a couple of related probability lemmas.

Lemma 12.6. Suppose that KF (β) holds. Then for any ε > 0, there exists η =
η(ε, β) > 0 such that the following holds for any 0 < δ < δ(ε, β). Suppose that (F, Y )
            THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                         97

is a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . Then
                       µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε CF (F)1−β/n δ −2β (δ 2 |F|)1−β/n .

Proof. Let F′ be the union of ⌈CF (F)⌉ independent random rigid motions of F, and Y ′
be the shading on F′ defined by the images of Y under these motions. By Lemma 12.5,
with high probability F′ is ⪅ 1 essentially distinct, CF (F′ ) ⪅ 1, and |F′ | ≈ CF (F)|F|.
Let F′′ be an essentially distinct subset of F′ such that (F′′ , Y ′ ) is a ⪆ 1 refinement
of (F′ , Y ′ ). Then CF (F′′ ) ⪅ CF (F′ ) ⪅ 1 and |F′′ | ⪅ CF (F)|F|. Applying KF (β) to
(F′′ , Y ′ ) with ε/2 in place of ε, we have
               µ(F, Y ) ≤ µ(F′ , Y ′ ) ⪅ µ(F′′ , Y ′ )
                                       ≤ δ −ε/2 δ −2β (δ 2 |F′′ |)1−β/n
                                       ⪅ δ −ε/2 CF (F)1−β/n δ −2β (δ 2 |F|)1−β/n ,
which immediately yields the desired bound.                                             □

  We point out that the analogue of Proposition 12.3 holds for Lemma 12.4 and
Lemma 12.6. For simplicity, we will often omit citing technical justifications like
Proposition 12.3 when applying the lemmas.

              13. Multiplicity estimates for slabs and planks

   In Section 4 we reduced the analysis of a general collection of convex sets in Rn to
that of two new families of convex sets: One is Katz–Tao, and the other is Frostman.
The estimates KKT (β) and KF (β) control the intersection multiplicity of sets of flakes
in Rn that are Katz–Tao and Frostman, respectively. But since the maximal density
factoring of flakes can be general planks, we also need to control the intersection
multiplicity of sets of planks that are Frostman or Katz–Tao. In this section, we
will show that incidence bounds for planks in Rn can be systematically reduced to
incidence bounds for flakes.
   Katz–Tao multiplicity estimates. Lemma 13.1 below says that the Kakeya
estimate KKT (β) implies an analogous estimate for planks of dimensions a × b × ⃗1.
Furthermore, a stronger estimate holds when a ≪ b, provided that the planks do not
concentrate into slabs (i.e., sets of dimensions θ × 1 × ⃗1 for θ ≪ 1). To make this
latter statement precise, we must define what it means for planks to concentrate into
slabs.
   If L is a set of planks in Rn of dimensions a × b × ⃗1, and S is a θ × 1 × ⃗1 slab with
θ ∈ [a/b, 1], then we define
(13.1)               LS := {L ∈ L : L ⊂ S and ∠(T L, T S) ≤ θ}.
In the above, ∠(T L, T S) refers to the angle between the hyperplanes T L, T S, where
T L is the hyperplane spanned by the (n − 1) longest axes of L, and similarly for S
(this is defined up to accuracy θ).
98           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

Lemma 13.1. Suppose that KKT (β) holds. Then for every ε > 0, there exists η, b0 >
0 such that the following holds for any 0 < a ≤ b ≤ b0 . Let L be a set of a × b × ⃗1
planks in B1 , and let Y be a shading on L with density λ(L, Y ) ≥ aη . Suppose that the
planks do not concentrate into slabs, in the following sense: There exists 0 ≤ γ ≤ 1
so that for each θ ∈ [a/b, 1] and every θ × 1 × ⃗1 slab S, |LS | ≤ a−η θγ |L|.
     If L is Katz–Tao, then µ(L) ≤ a−ε ( ab )γβ |L|β . More generally, we have
                                                          γβ
                                      −ε                  a
(13.2)                      µ(L) ≤ a ∆max (L)      1−β
                                                                 |L|β .
                                                          b
Remark 13.2. We will only apply Lemma 13.1 in the special cases γ = 0 (for which
the nonconcentration condition is vacuous, and can be ignored), and γ = 1. When L
is Katz–Tao and γ = 0, the RHS of (13.2) is ≲ a−ε |L|β , which matches the estimate
KKT (β) for flakes in Definition 12.1.

Remark 13.3. The inequality automatically holds in the case when µ(L) ≤ a−η . To
see this, note that the nonconcentration hypothesis gives that |L| ≥ aη (b/a)γ (by
taking θ = a/b), and η is much less than ε .

  Frostman multiplicity estimates. Lemma 13.4 below says that the Kakeya
estimate KF (β) implies an analogous estimate for planks of dimensions a × b × ⃗1,
provided we avoid the following bad scenario. Let L be a set of planks of dimensions
a × b × ⃗1, L ∈ L, and θ ∈ [a/b, 1]. We define the thickened plank Lθ := Nθb (L), which
has dimensions θb × b × ⃗1. If Lθ contains a very large number of planks from L, then
this can cause an unacceptably large intersection multiplicity µ(L). The next result
says that this is the only obstruction to obtaining an analogue of KF (β) for planks.

Lemma 13.4. Suppose that KF (β) holds. Then for every ε > 0, there exists η, b0 > 0
such that the following holds for any 0 < a ≤ b ≤ b0 . Let L be a set of a × b × ⃗1

aη . Suppose that the planks do not concentrate into thickened planks, in the following
sense: There is a number M ≥ 1 so that for each θ ∈ [a/b, 1] and every L ∈ L, we
have |L[Lθ ]| ≤ M θ. Then
                         |U (L)| ≥ aε CF (L)β/n−1 b2β (M −1 b2 |L|)β/n ,
and
                                                  a
                      µ(L) ≤ a−ε CF (L)1−β/n M β/n b−2β (b2 |L|)1−β/n .
                                                  b
Remark 13.5. Both inequalities automatically hold in the case when µ(L) ≤ a−η . To
                                                             1
see this, note that M ≥ b/a (by taking θ = a/b), CF (L) ≳ |L|ab , and η is much less
than ε.

  Flakes that factor through planks. Lemmas 13.1 and 13.4 allow us to obtain
improved multiplicity bounds for families of flakes that factor through planks. The
precise statement is as follows.

  Note that the analogue of Proposition 12.3 applies to Lemmas 13.1 and 13.4, and

13.1. Slabs. A standard L2 method gives strong bounds for the incidences of slabs.
For example, Lemma 4.7 gives the nice Gr(n, n − 1) version of Theorem 1.1 in a fairly
easy way, and has been used many times so far. In this subsection, we state another
two simple variants of Lemma 4.7, which will be useful in the remaining parts of this
paper.
   A key character is the notion of “typical intersection angle”, which is meaningfully
defined up to accuracy δ. Suppose that S is a set of δ × 1 × ⃗1 slabs with a shading
Y . Define the set of incidence triples
                      T ri(S) = {(x, S1 , S2 ) : x ∈ Y (S1 ) ∩ Y (S2 )}.
There is a natural measure on T ri(S) coming from the volume measure for x and the
counting measure for S1 , S2 . By Definition 3.4 with k = n − 1, we can define the
angle between two slabs S1 , S2 as their major angle (well-defined up to additive error
δ). Then we define
                   T riθ (S) = {(x, S1 , S2 ) ∈ T ri(S) : ∠(S1 , S2 ) ∼ θ}.
Definition 13.7. We say that θ is a typical angle of intersection for S if |T riθ (S)| ≈
|T ri(S)| up to log(1/δ) factors. If µ(S) ≥ 2, then S always has a typical intersection
angle θ ∈ [δ, 1].
Lemma 13.8. Suppose S is a set of δ × 1 × ⃗1 slabs contained in S̃, a θ × 1 × ⃗1 slab,
with δ ≤ θ ≤ 1. Suppose the slabs of S are shaded with λ(S, Y ) ≥ δ η . If θ is a typical
intersection angle for S, then |U (S, Y )| ≳ δ 2η |S̃|.

Proof. Exactly the same as that of [14, Lemma 6.8], and so omitted.                      □

  We also have the following analogue of Lemma 4.7, which has some flexibility in
applications.
100         CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

Lemma 13.9. Suppose S is a set of δ × b1 × · · · × bn−1 convex sets in B1 ⊂ Rn ,
and Y is a shading on S with λ(S, Y ) ≥ δ η . Suppose also bj ≥ δ η for each j. If
∆max (S) ≤ δ −η , then µ(S, Y ) ≲ δ −Cn η . If CF (S) ≤ δ −η , then |U (S, Y )| ≳ δ Cn η . Here
Cn is a constant depending only on n.

Proof. Similar to Lemma 4.7, and so omitted.                                                □

13.2. Planks. Next we suppose that L is a set of a × b × ⃗1 planks in B1 . We will
study L by relating it to incidence problems involving slabs and flakes. The incidence
problem for slabs is solved completely using Lemma 13.8. Assuming KKT (β) or
KF (β), we get information about the incidence problem for flakes. In this way, we
are able to prove estimates for µ(L) based on various geometric assumptions about
L.
   The heuristic picture behind the whole process is very similar to that explained
in [14, Section 6.2]. Given two intersecting planks, L1 , L2 , define the angle between
them as the angle between the hyperplanes T L1 , T L2 , where T L is the hyperplane
spanned by the (n − 1) longest axes of L. If the planks L1 and L2 have dimensions
a × b × ⃗1, then this angle is meaningfully defined up to accuracy a/b. Let θ denote a
“typical” angle of intersection for L.
   First, we note that if the angle between L1 and L2 is ≫ θ, then their intersection
will not play an important role. Therefore, we can subdivide L into smaller collections
of planks as follows. If S is a θ × 1 × ⃗1 slab in B1 , we define LS as in (13.1). We can
                            F
now decompose L as L = S LS , where S varies over some essentially distinct slabs.
If θ is a typical angle of intersection of (L, Y ), then morally the sets U (LS , Y ) are
disjoint and we can study them separately. Fix a single S and we will analyze LS .
   Next, let Q denote a θb × b × b⃗1 box, and let LQ be the set of planks L ∈ LS
so that L ∩ Q has dimensions a × b × b⃗1. In other words, LQ are the planks that
intersect Q “tangentially”, making the intersection as large as possible. Assuming
that µ(L) ≫ 1 and assuming that LQ is not empty, then morally Lemma 13.8 tells us
that |U (LS , Y ) ∩ Q| ≈ |Q|. For each plank L ∈ LS , the θb-neighborhood of L can be
covered by boxes Q as above, and so we see that morally most of this neighborhood
is contained in U (LS , Y ). This motivates the definition of the thickened version of
LS as follows.

Definition 13.10. For each L ∈ L, define Lθ to be the θb-neighborhood of L. Define
Lθ to be a set of essentially distinct prisms of the form Lθ , so that each L′ ∈ L is
contained in a set Lθ ∈ Lθ for which L′θ is comparable to Lθ . Note that if Lθ and
L′θ are comparable, then they are contained in comparable slabs S and S ′ . Thus Lθ
                                                   S
decomposes into a boundedly overlapping union S Lθ,S , where the union is taken
over incomparable slabs of dimensions θ × 1 × ⃗1.

  In the previous paragraph, we saw that if θ is a typical angle of intersection for L,
then morally U (LS ) is almost the same as U (Lθ,S ).
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                        101

unit ball and takes each Lθ ∈ Lθ,S to a b-flake. Therefore, the problem of estimating
|U (Lθ,S )| is equivalent to a problem about b-flakes which we can bound using KKT (β)
and/or KF (β).
   The following two lemmas give a precise version of the moral statements above.
First we carefully define the typical angle of intersection.

Lemma 13.11 instead of [14, Lemma 6.11] to find the typical angle of intersection θ,
102        CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

and apply Lemma 13.8 instead of [14, Lemma 6.8] to deal with intersection of local
a × b × b⃗1 slabs.                                                              □

Remark 13.14. If (L, Y ) has a typical angle of intersection θ, then Lemma 13.13 holds
for this value of θ.

   By a linear change of variables, we can convert Lθ,S from a set of θb × b × ⃗1 planks
in S into a set F of b-flakes in B1 . To see this more carefully, recall that Lθ,S consists
of θb × b × ⃗1 planks L in the slab S so that T L is tangent to T S. The slab S has
dimensions θ × 1 × ⃗1. Let ϕ : S → B1 be a linear change of variables. Because of the
tangency condition on L, ϕ(L) has dimensions b × b × ⃗1.
  To summarize, we have reduced the original incidence problem for L to an incidence
problem for Lθ,S , which is equivalent to an incidence problem for b-flakes F. If we
assume KKT (β) or KF (β), then we can get bounds for µ(F) and hence bounds for
µ(L). We are now ready to prove Lemmas 13.1 and 13.4.

13.4. A Frostman multiplicity estimate.

Proof of Lemma 13.4. The arguments closely follow those in [14, Section 6.4]. An
additional twist is that the exponents in β need to be altered accordingly to match
those in Lemma 12.6. We may suppose that µ(L, Y ) ≥ a−η , since otherwise we are
done by Remark 13.5. Apply Lemma 13.13. Abusing notation, we will continue to
refer to the resulting refinement by (L, Y ), and we write LS and Lθ,S in place of L′S
and L′θ,S .
   By Lemma 13.13, there is a number θ ∈ [a/b, 1] and a set S of θ × 1 × ⃗1 slabs so
that
                             |U (L, Y )| ⪆ |S| |U (Lθ,S , Yθ,S )|
for some S ∈ S.
  Lemma 13.13 also gives a density lower bound
                                     λ(Lθ,S , Yθ,S ) ⪆ a4η .

   Define C = CF (L, B1 ). This does not imply that CF (Lθ,S , S) ≤ C. So we have to
work out what we know about Lθ,S . Let Lθ be formed by thickening the planks of
L to dimensions θb × b × ⃗1. By Lemma 13.13 and our nonconcentration assumption,
there is a number N ≤ M θ so that each plank Lθ ∈ Lθ contains ∼ N planks of L
(recall that we have replaced L by a ≈ 1 refinement, but the refinement preserves the
upper bound |L[Lθ ]| ≤ M θ).
  By Proposition 4.3(A), CF (Lθ , B1 ) ⪅ CF (L, B1 ) = C, and so
                               |Lθ,S | ≤ |Lθ [S]| ⪅ Cθ|Lθ |.

  The set Lθ,S is not necessarily Frostman, but since Lθ is Frostman, we do know
that for any convex K ⊂ S,
                  ∆(Lθ,S , K) ≤ ∆(Lθ , K) ⪅ C∆(Lθ , B1 ) ∼ C|Lθ ||Lθ |.
104         CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

  Since |Lθ | ≈ |S| |Lθ,S |,
                                             |Lθ | |Lθ,S |
                  ∆(Lθ,S , K) ⪅ C|S||S|                    = C|S||S| ∆(Lθ,S , S).
                                                 |S|
Therefore CF (Lθ,S , S) ⪅ C|S||S|.
  Now by a linear change of variables, we can convert Lθ,S to a set of b-flakes F in B1 .
Let YF be the image of the shading Yθ,S under this change of variables. Since convex
sets are invariant with respect to a linear change of variables, CF (F) ⪅ C|S||S|. Also,
λ(F, YF ) = λ(Lθ,S , Yθ,S ) ⪆ a4η .
  If η and b0 are chosen sufficiently small depending on ε, then by Lemma 12.6 (noting
Proposition 12.3) to (F, YF ) with ε/2 in place of ε, we have

                                F |Y (F )|
                               P
                   |U (F)| =
                                µ(F, Y )
                               ε/2+4η
                           ⪆a           (C|S||S|)β/n−1 b2β (b2 |F|)β/n
                           ≈ aε/2+4η C β/n−1 (|S||S|)−1 b2β (b2 |S||Lθ |)β/n
                           = aε/2+4η C β/n−1 (|S||S|)−1 b2β (b2 θ|L|/N )β/n
                           ≥ aε/2+4η C β/n−1 (|S||S|)−1 b2β (M −1 b2 |L|)β/n .

  Therefore, if b0 > 0 is sufficiently small, then we have

       |U (L)| ⪆ |S||U (Lθ,S )| ∼ |S||S||U (F)| ⪆ aε/2+4η C β/n−1 b2β (M −1 b2 |L|)β/n .

This gives the desired lower bound for |U (L)|. To get an upper bound for µ(L),
we relate µ(L) to |U (L)|. This part is just algebra and we leave the details to the
reader.                                                                           □

                           14. A multiscale decomposition

   In order to apply Theorem 6.2, our collection of flakes must be Frostman at every
scale or Katz–Tao at every scale, respectively. Our main result in this section says
that either this is true, or else there is a long sequence of scales that are far from being
Frostman (resp. Katz–Tao). It is reminiscent of [14, Lemma 7.7] but is much more
complicated. The reason is that to make the application of multiscale decompositions
in Section 15 and 16 legitimate, one needs to preserve lower bounds of some convex
Wolff constants under the maximal density factoring (Lemma 4.5). Also, we want
certain “strongly non-sticky” property to be preserved under the multiplicity factoring
(Lemma 5.4). These are not guaranteed by a version like [14, Lemma 7.7].15
  Before everything, let us first state a simple variant of Lemma 4.5:

Lemma 14.1. Let F be a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn . Then there
is a set F′ ⊂ F with |F′ | ⪆ |F|; a family W of convex sets in Rn , each of approximately
the same dimensions; and a partition
                                         F′ =
                                                 G
                                                       FW ,
                                                W ∈W

where each FW is a uniform set contained in W . Moreover,
    (i) ∆max (W) ≲ 1.
   (ii) For each W ∈ W, we have CF (FW , W ) ⪅ 1 and ∆(FW , W ) ≈ ∆max (F′ ).
  15This technicality is nontrivial and also appeared in [14], but was neglected therein.
108           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

      (iii) For any two scales δ ≤ τ ≤ θ ≤ 1, there exist constants U1 , U2 , U3 , such that
            for any W ∈ W, we have
                |(FW )τ [Fθ ]| ≈ U1 , CF ((FW )τ [Fθ ]) ≈ U2 , ∆max ((FW )τ [Fθ ]) ≈ U3
          for all Fθ ∈ Fθ with (FW )τ [Fθ ] ̸= ∅.

  Note that Item (iii) above is stronger than the mere uniformity of each FW : It
requires the uniformized data
                        |(FW )τ [Fθ ]|, CF ((FW )τ [Fθ ]), ∆max ((FW )τ [Fθ ])
to be essentially the same for all W ∈ W. The proof of Lemma 14.1 goes as follows.
We first use Lemma 4.5 to get an initial partition, and then apply Proposition 11.1 to
each FW in the output. These give the uniformity of each FW and Items (i) and (ii).
Item (iii) can be easily obtained by applying another uniformization procedure to the
uniformized data of different FW , which also preserves Items (i) and (ii). We omit
the details.

Definition 14.2. We call a set of δ-flakes F weakly uniform at scales [τ0 , θ0 ] ⊂ [δ, 1]
up to a factor C, if for any τ0 ≤ τ ≤ θ ≤ θ0 , there exist constants U1 , U2 , U3 , such
that
 |Fτ [Fθ ]| ∈ [C −1 U1 , CU1 ], CF (Fτ [Fθ ]) ∈ [C −1 U2 , CU2 ], ∆max (Fτ [Fθ ]) ∈ [C −1 U3 , CU3 ]
for all Fθ ∈ Fθ .

  The benefit of Lemma 14.1 is that we have strong uniformity for each FW , while
keeping the factoring properties essentially unchanged. For convenience, we introduce
the following definition, which is a variant of Definition 4.6.

Definition 14.3. Let F be a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn , and let
W be a family of convex sets in Rn . Suppose that there is a partition F = W ∈W FW .
                                                                          F

We say that W uniformly factors F if the following is true:
        • ∆max (W) ≲ 1.
        • Each set W ∈ W has approximately the same dimensions.
        • For each W ∈ W, we have CF (FW , W ) ⪅ 1 and ∆(FW , W ) ≈ ∆max (F).
        • Item (iii) in Lemma 14.1 holds.

  Thus Lemma 14.1 says that W uniformly factors F′ .
  For any set A, let 2A be the power set of A, i.e., the collection of all subsets of A.

a map ΦF,ρ : 2F → 2Fρ a λ∗ -dense selection map at scale ρ, if for any F ⊂ F, we have
ΦF,ρ (F) ⊆ Fρ , and | Fρ ∈ΦF,ρ (F) F[Fρ ]| ≥ λ∗ |F|.

   Lemma 14.5 is often called the “multiscale decomposition”, and is the key tool for
the reduction we want. Lemma 14.5 (A) will be used for the proof of Main Lemma
1, and Lemma 14.5 (B) will be used for the proof of Main Lemma 2, as we will see in
the next two sections.

                         15. The proof of Main Lemma 1

  In this section, we sketch the proof of Main Lemma 1. Recall the statement:
Main Lemma 1. KKT (β) implies KF (β).
  Let us first digest what the lemma is saying. Suppose that F is a set of δ-flakes in
B1 with CF (F) ≤ δ −η . We have to prove the bound
(15.1)                       µ(F) ≤ δ −ε (δ −2 )β (δ 2 |F|)1−β/n .

   Since CF (F, B1 ) ≤ δ −η , we have |F| ≥ δ −2+η . If |F| ≈ δ −2 , then CF (F, B1 ) ≤ δ −η
is equivalent to ∆max (F) ⪅ δ −η , and then KKT (β) gives µ(F) ≤ δ −ε δ −2β , which is the
desired bound. So the case |F| ≈ δ −2 is trivial, and the content of the lemma is in
the case when |F| ≫ δ −2 .
  If |F| ≫ δ −2 , then ∆max (F) ≫ 1, so we cannot immediately apply KKT (β) to
F. If F̃ ⊂ F is a random subset with |F̃| ∼ δ −2 , then it is not hard to check that
∆max (F̃) ⪅ 1. We can apply KKT (β) to F̃, and we get µ(F̃) ⪅ δ −2β which is equivalent
to |U (F̃)| ⪆ δ 2β . We have |U (F)| ≥ |U (F̃)|, and this translates into
(15.2)                            µ(F) ⪅ (δ −2 )β (δ 2 |F|).

   Comparing (15.2) with our goal (15.1), we see that we need to reduce the exponent
of the last factor from 1 to 1 − β/n. In our proof of (n − 2)-plane Kakeya in Rn , any
exponent strictly less than 1 will work.
Remark 15.1. A similar observation has been pointed out in [14] for line Kakeya in R3 ,
in which the exponent can be reduced to 1−β/2. In contrast, here we can only expect
a worse improvement, which makes our modified formulation of KF (β) different from
that in [14, Definition 3.5]. This ultimately comes down to the distinction between the
dimension of sets of directional spaces: There are at most ∼ δ −2 essentially distinct
δ-tubes passing through a fixed point in R3 , while there are at most ∼ δ −2(n−2)
essentially distinct δ-flakes passing through a fixed point in Rn . In other words,
pointwise the flakes can be much more concentrated than tubes. Our Theorem 1.1
shows that such much worse behavior still does not occur on average. The technical
reason is that any exponent less than 1 suffices for our purpose.

   We can also describe the lemma in terms of |U (F)|, which may be more intuitive.
Since we assume KKT (β), we already know that if CF (F) ⪅ 1 and |F| ∼ δ −2 , then
|U (F)| ⪆ δ 2β . Now we need to show that if CF (F) ⪅ 1 and if |F| is far larger than δ −2 ,
then |U (F)| is a little larger than δ 2β . While this may sound intuitive, it is not at all
trivial to prove, and the proof will depend crucially on (n − 2)-plane sticky Kakeya.
120           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

    We organize the proof as a bootstrapping or self-improving argument.

Lemma 15.2. Suppose that KKT (β) holds and that KF (γ) holds for some γ > β.
Then KF (γ − ν) holds for ν = ν(γ, β) > 0. For β fixed, ν = ν(γ, β) > 0 is monotone
in γ.

  Since KKT (1) and KF (n − 1) trivially hold, Lemma 15.2 clearly implies Main
Lemma 1.

Proof of Lemma 15.2. By Definition 12.2, to prove KF (γ − ν), we let F be a set of
essentially distinct δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn with CF (F, B1 ) ≤ δ −η , and Y be a shading
on F with λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . We need to bound µ(F, Y ) or |U (F, Y )|. To begin with, let
(F′ , Y ) be a ⪆ 1 refinement of (F, Y ) so that there is λ > 0 such that |Y (F )|/|F | ∼ λ
for all F ∈ F′ . Then |F′ | ⪆ δ η |F|, CF (F′ ) ⪅ δ −η CF (F) ≤ δ −2η , and λ ∼ λ(F′ , Y ) ⪆
λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
   Let ε0 and δ0 be the output of Theorem 6.2 with γ/n in place of ε. Let N ≥
max{4(4n/ε0 )5 , 105 } be an integer to be chosen later, and define ε = (10/N )1/5 ≤
ε0 /4n. Let η ≤ η0 ≤ η1 ≤ . . . ≤ ηN ≤ ε to be chosen later. Each ηj−1 will be selected
very small depending on ηj , ε, and β.

                                  16. The proof of Main Lemma 2

  Our last goal is to prove Main Lemma 2. Recall the statement:
  Main Lemma 2. If KKT (β) and KF (β) hold, then KKT (β − ν) holds, where
ν = ν(β) > 0 is monotone.

16.1. Reduction to the strongly non-sticky case. The (n − 2)-plane sticky
Kakeya theorem plays a crucial role in this proof. By using Theorem 6.2(B), we
are able to reduce the problem to a situation which is not sticky in a strong sense.

Lemma 16.1 (Very not sticky case of Main Lemma 2). For all β > 0, there is some
εscal = εscal (β) ∈ (0, 1/2) so that for all ζ > 0, there is some ν = ν(β, ζ) > 0 and
η = η(β, ζ) > 0 so that the following is true for small δ:
  If KKT (β) and KF (β) hold and if (F, Y ) is a uniform set of δ-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn
obeying
       • ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η and λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
       • For each ρ ∈ [δ 1−εscal , δ εscal ], |Fρ | ≥ ρ−2−ζ .
Then
                                                  µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ ν |F|β .
126           CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

  For technical reasons, let us first show that Lemma 16.1 immediately implies a
seemingly stronger version of it, which only requires weak uniformity up to a small
factor:

Lemma 16.2. For all β > 0, there is some εscal = εscal (β) ∈ (0, 1/2) so that for all
ζ > 0, there is some ν = ν(β, ζ) > 0 and η = η(β, ζ) > 0 so that the following is true
for small δ:
  If KKT (β) and KF (β) hold and if (F, Y ) is a set of essentially distinct δ-flakes in
B1 ⊂ Rn obeying
       • For each ρ ∈ [δ, 1], there is Nρ > 0 such that

                                        δ η Nρ ≤ |F[Fρ ]| ≤ δ −η Nρ

         for all Fρ ∈ Fρ .
       • ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η and λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η .
       • For each ρ ∈ [δ 1−εscal , δ εscal ], |Fρ | ≥ ρ−2−ζ .
Then
                                              µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ ν |F|β .

Proof. Note that our assumptions imply |F| ≥ |Fρ |δ η Nρ , and so Nρ ≤ δ −η |F|/|Fρ |.
  Let (F′ , Y ) be a ⪆ 1 refinement of (F, Y ) so that |Y (F )|/|F | is constant for all
F ∈ F′ . By Proposition 11.1, there is a uniform subset F′′ ⊂ F′ , with |F′′ | ⪆ |F′ |.
Then (F′′ , Y ) is a ⪆ 1 refinement of (F′ , Y ) (thus of (F, Y )). As in Section 2.4, by
applying dyadic pigeonholing to the shading Y , we find a further uniform ⪆ refinement
(F′′ , Y ′ ) of (F, Y ). Note that λ(F′′ , Y ′ ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η , and by Proposition 4.3(B),
we have ∆max (F′′ ) ≤ ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η .
  Since
                         |F′′ ||F | ≥                          |Y (F )| ≥ δ η |F||F |,
                                        X                  X
                                              |Y (F )| ⪆
                                        F′′                F
             ′′
we have |F | ⪆ δ |F|. Now the key observation is that for each ρ ∈ [δ, 1] and Fρ ∈ F′′ρ ,
                    η

we have
                            F′′ [Fρ ] ≤ |F[Fρ ]| ≤ δ −η Nρ ≤ δ −2η |F|/|Fρ |,
which by a simple counting argument implies

                        |F′′ρ | ≥ |F′′ |/(δ −2η |F|/|Fρ |) ⪆ δ 3η |Fρ | ≥ δ 3η ρ−2−ζ .

  For each β > 0, let εscal = εscal (β) be as in Lemma 16.1. For each ρ ∈ [δ 1−εscal , δ εscal ],
we have

                                  |F′′ρ | ≥ δ 4η ρ−2−ζ ≥ ρ−2−ζ+4η/εscal .

Then for any ζ > 0, take ν = ν(β, ζ/2)/2 > 0 and η = min{εscal ζ/8, η(β, ζ/2)/2} > 0,
where the binary functions ν(·, ·) and η(·, ·) are as in Lemma 16.1. One can easily

verify that ∆max (F′′ ) ≤ δ −η(β,ζ/2) , λ(F′′ , Y ′ ) ≥ δ η(β,ζ/2) , and |F′′ρ | ≥ ρ−2−ζ/2 for each

                    µ(F, Y ) ⪅ µ(F′′ , Y ′ ) ≤ δ ν(β,ζ/2) |F′′ |β ≤ δ ν(β,ζ/2) |F|β .
So µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ ν |F|β , as desired.                                                            □

   Our goal in this subsection is to show that Lemma 16.2 implies Main Lemma 2.
The proof is similar to the proof of Main Lemma 1, using Lemma 14.5(B), Proposi-
tion 13.6(B), and Theorem 6.2(B) instead of their Part (A) counterparts.
   Before formally beginning the proof, let us first do some two-ends reduction, which
allows us to only consider the case when F is broad (thus |F| is large). For this
purpose, we need the following analogues of [27, Definition 7.6 and 7.7]:
Definition 16.3. Let ε, δ > 0 and C ≥ 1. We say that a multiset V ⊂ Gr(n, k) is
ε-broad with error C at scales ≥ δ, if for all V0 ∈ V and all r ∈ [δ, 1], we have
                             |{V ∈ V : ∠(V, V0 ) ≤ r}| ≤ Crε |V|.

  More generally, let V be contained in a metric ball21 B ⊂ Gr(n, k) of radius ρ. We
say that V is ε-broad with error C at scales ≥ δ inside B, if for all V0 ∈ V and all
r ∈ [δ, ρ], we have
                           |{V ∈ V : ∠(V, V0 ) ≤ r}| ≤ C(r/ρ)ε |V|.
Definition 16.4. Let (F, Y ) be a set of (δ, k)-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn and their associated
shading.
   (A) We say that (F, Y ) is ε-broad with error C if for each x ∈ U (F, Y ), the set
       {dir(F ) : x ∈ Y (F )} is ε-broad with error C at scales ≥ δ.
   (B) We say that (F, Y ) is ε-broad with error C relative to Fρ 22 (ρ ∈ [δ, 1]) if for
       each Fρ ∈ Fρ , the image of (F[Fρ ], Y ) under the rescaling taking Fρ to B1 , as
       a set of (δ/ρ)-flakes in B1 , is ε-broad with error C.

  The key observation here is that by Remark 3.5 and Lemma 3.6, the metric on
Gr(n, k) has the doubling property and is compatible with coarse coverings of flakes,
and so all the discussions in [27, Section 7.1] can be carried out in parallel with
Gr(n, k) in place of Sn−1 and F in place of T. In particular, we have the following
analogue of [27, Corollary 7.10]:
Lemma 16.5. Let ε > 0, and let (F, Y ) be a set of (δ, k)-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn . Then
there exists a scale ρ ∈ [δ, 1] and a ⪆ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) of (F, Y ), so that the
following holds:
    (i) Each point x ∈ Rn is contained in ⪅ ρ−ε sets of the form U (F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ ) where
        Fρ ∈ Fρ .
  21Recall from Remark 3.5 that the metric is induced by the major angle.
  22A set of (ρ, k)-flakes with the smallest cardinality with F = S
                                                                       Fρ ∈Fρ F[Fρ ].
128            CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

       (ii) F′ is ε-broad with error ≈ 1 relative to F′ρ .
      (iii) λ(F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ) for all Fρ ∈ F′ρ .

Proof. The proof is exactly the same as that of [27, Corollary 7.10] – only harmless
constants will be changed – and so omitted for simplicity. We only point out Item (iii)
comes from dyadic pigeonholing on the average density inside each Fρ in the middle
of the proof.                                                                        □

  Now let us turn to the two-ends reduction. By Definition 12.1, to prove KKT (β −ν),
we take any set of δ-flakes (F, Y ) in B1 ⊂ Rn with ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η and λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η ,
and need to find for any ε > 0 some ν = ν(β) > 0 such that
                                            µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε |F|β−ν
holds when η and δ are small enough. Since ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η implies that F is essen-
tially distinct up to multiplicity ≲ δ −η , by a harmless pigeonholing argument, we may
only consider the case that F is essentially distinct. Also, we may assume ε < 1/4.
  Fix 0 < ε < 1/4. By applying Lemma 16.5 to (F, Y ) with ε/2 in place of ε therein,
we get a scale ρ ∈ [δ, 1] and a ⪆ 1 refinement (F′ , Y ′ ) of (F, Y ), so that
        (i) Each point x ∈ Rn is contained in ⪅ ρ−ε/2 sets of the form U (F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ ) where
            Fρ ∈ Fρ .
       (ii) (F′ , Y ′ ) is (ε/2)-broad with error ≈ 1 relative to F′ρ .
      (iii) λ(F′ [Fρ ], Y ′ ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η for all Fρ ∈ F′ρ .
Note that Item (i) implies that

Proof of the claim. By pigeonholing, there exists F0 ∈ F̃ with |Ỹ (F0 )| ⪆ δ η |F0 | ≳
δ̃ 2+4nη/ε . By Definition 16.4, Item (ii) means that (F̃, Ỹ ) is (ε/2)-broad with error
≈ 1, i.e., for each x ∈ U (F̃, Ỹ ), the set {dir(F ) : x ∈ Ỹ (F )} is (ε/2)-broad with error
≈ 1 at scales ≥ δ̃. In particular, by Definition 16.3 with V0 = dir(F0 ), we know that
for each x ∈ Ỹ (F0 ), we have

                       |{F ∈ F̃ : x ∈ Ỹ (F ), ∠(F, F0 ) ≤ r}| ⪅ rε/2 |F̃Ỹ (x)|

for all r ∈ [δ̃, 1]. By taking r = δ̃ ε , we see that for each x ∈ Ỹ (F0 ), there exists some
Fx ∈ F̃ such that ∠(Fx , F0 ) ≥ δ̃ ε , and so |(10Fx ) ∩ F0 | ≲ δ̃ 3 /∠(Fx , F0 ) ≤ δ̃ 3−ε by
Lemma 3.7. Now we can iteratively pick Fx as follows until we cannot continue any
more: Fix any x1 ∈ Ỹ (F0 ), then for each j ≥ 2, we pick xj ∈ Ỹ (F0 )\ j−1
                                                                            S
                                                                              i=1 (10Fxi ). Note
that {Fxj }j ⊂ F̃ are essentially distinct and has cardinality at least |Ỹ (F0 )|/δ̃ 3−ε ⪆
δ̃ −1+4nη/ε+ε ≥ δ̃ −3/4+4nη/ε . Thus |F̃| ≥ δ̃ −1/2 for η ≤ ε/(32n).                          □

  Suppose we know that KKT (β − ν) holds for some ν = ν(β) > 0 and all δ-flakes
(F, Y ) that additionally satisfy |F| ≥ δ −1/2 , then by taking η ≪ ε and applying
KKT (β − ν) to (F̃, Ỹ ) (Definition 12.1, with δ̃ in place of δ and ε/4 in place of ε), we
have

         µ(F′ [Fρ∗ ], Y ′ ) = µ(F̃, Ỹ ) ≤ δ̃ −ε/4 |F̃|β−ν = δ̃ −ε/4 |F′ [Fρ∗ ]|β−ν ≤ δ −ε/4 |F′ |β−ν ,

which implies

         µ(F, Y ) ⪅ µ(F′ , Y ′ ) ⪅ ρ−ε/2 µ(F′ [Fρ∗ ], Y ′ ) ≤ δ −3ε/4 |F′ |β−ν ≤ δ −3ε/4 |F|β−ν ,

and so µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε |F|β−ν , as desired.
  Therefore, from now on, we shall only focus on essentially distinct F with |F| ≥

δ   , which is the most interesting case and requires using Lemma 16.2.

Remark 16.6. This largeness reduction is technically necessary for the forthcoming
proof to be entirely rigorous. If we do not have any additional assumption on |F|,
then via similar arguments we can still get estimates of the form

(16.1)                                    µ(F, Y ) ≤ δ −ε |F|β−ν .

This is superficially sufficient, but actually not, as ν depends on ε if one carefully
tracks the dependence of the parameters. This issue also happened in their stream-
lined proof paper [14, Section 9.1], but was neglected. However, we borrow the idea
of the two-end reduction from Wang and Zahl’s original paper [27, Section 7.1] to
resolve this subtle problem.

16.2. Maximal density factoring revisited. It remains to prove Lemma 16.1.
For technical reasons, we will need the following variation of Lemma 4.5 with biased
choices:

Lemma 16.7 ([14, Lemma 9.2], Biased maximal density factoring lemma). Let V
be a finite family of convex subsets of Rn contained in a convex set U , and suppose

  One may consult [14, Section 9.2] for the motivation behind Lemma 16.7. We will

serve that we can still apply Proposition 5.1 to the factoring W VW —Proposition 5.1

16.3. Setup for the proof of Lemma 16.1. To prove Lemma 16.1, we will study
how F behaves in smaller balls at various scales. The proof is similar to that in
[14, Section 9] and does not contain new insights, except for some modifications to
handle flakes instead of tubes. For simplicity, we only provide a sketch of the proof
by omitting details about technical shading manipulations.
  Our goal is to prove that

(16.22)                              µ(F, Y ) ⪅ δ ν |F|β ,

for some ν = ν(β, ζ) > 0. For this purpose, it also suffices to show that

(16.23)                         |U (F, Y )| ⪆ δ −ν |F ||F|1−β ,

for some ν = ν(β, ζ) > 0.
   The argument has several cases. In some cases, we will prove (16.22) and in some
cases we will prove (16.23). In some of the cases, we will prove (16.23) by showing
that U (F, Y ) fills a larger than expected fraction of a typical a-ball that intersects
U (F, Y ), and we will pair this with an estimate on U (Fa ).
   We have to do some pigeonholing to prepare for our argument. First we refine
(F, Y ) to be (F, Y ′ ) so that for each x ∈ U (F, Y ′ ), the set FY ′ (x) is uniform. We
identify FY ′ (x) with a subset of Gr(n, n − 2) corresponding to the direction subspaces
of the flakes. Then for each x, we can choose a uniform subset Ux of FY ′ (x) with
|Ux | ⪆ |FY ′ (x)|. We define Y ′ so that x ∈ Y ′ (F ) if and only if F ∈ Ux . This is a ⪆ 1
refinement. Because of the uniformity, there is a function µ(ρ) so that for any F0 ∈ F
and x ∈ Y ′ (F0 ),

(16.24)                  #{F ∈ FY ′ (x) : ∠(F, F0 ) ≤ ρ} ≈ µ(ρ),

  In particular, we have µ(F, Y ) ⪅ µ(F, Y ′ ) ≈ µ(1). Moreover, for each Fρ ∈ Fρ , we
have
                                   µ(F[Fρ ], Y ′ ) ≈ µ(ρ).
Abusing notation, we will refer to Y ′ as Y from here on.
  Next we apply Lemma 5.4 to (F, Y ) and Fa , for a = δ ηj , j = 1, . . . , ⌊η −1 ⌋. We
obtain a ≈ 1 refinement of (F, Y ) (abusing notation, we will continue to refer to this
136          CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

as (F, Y )) so that for each x ∈ U (Fa , YFa ), we have
                                                              |U (F, Y ) ∩ B(x, a)|
(16.25)                     |U (F, Y )| ⪆ |U (Fa , YFa )|                           .
                                                                    |B(x, a)|
  Recall that λ(Fa , YFa ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ) ≥ δ η . We also have
                                                                   (a/δ)2
                                           ∆max (Fa ) ≤ δ −η                .
                                                                   |F[Fa ]|
Applying Lemma 12.4 to (Fa , YFa ) with ν/10 in place of ε, we have (provided η > 0
is chosen sufficiently small)
(16.26)
                                                                                 !
                                                       (a/δ)2
                                                                  1−β                           2β
                                                                                                 a
                                                  
                            ν/9                                              β            ν/9
      |U (Fa , YFa )| ≥ δ         (|Fa ||Fa |)                           |Fa |       ≈δ                 |F ||F|1−β .
                                                       |F[Fa ]|                                  δ
Chaining (16.25) and (16.26), we conclude that in order to obtain our desired estimate
(16.23), it suffices to prove that there exists an a-ball Ba with
                                                                      2β
                                         |U (F) ∩ Ba |         δ
(16.27)                                                ⪆ δ −2ν                   .
                                             |Ba |             a
   With these preparations, we are ready to set up the main objects in the proof. Let
r1 = δ εscal . Divide U (Fr1 , Y ) into boundedly overlapping balls B of radius r1 .
   For each ball B, we define FB to be a set of essentially distinct δ × δ × r1⃗1 flake
segments contained in B of the form F ∩ B with F ∈ F. For each FB ∈ FB , we let
F(FB ) be the set of flakes F ∈ F so that F ∩ B is comparable to FB .
   First, we define a shading on FB . For each FB ∈ FB , we consider the multiplicity
|YF(FB ) (x)|. We choose YB (FB ) ⊂ FB by pigeonholing the multiplicity |YF(FB ) (x)|.
Next, we refine Y so that if F ∈ F(FB ), Y (F ) ∩ FB ⊂ YB (FB ). This is a ⪆ 1
refinement. Finally, we refine (FB , YB ) by pigeonholing |YB (FB )|. Again we refine Y
so that Y (F ) ∩ FB ⊂ YB (FB ) and this is still a ⪆ 1 refinement.
   We let B be the set of balls B with λ(FB , YB ) ⪆ λ(F, Y ). Then we refine Y by
removing the parts of Y (F ) that are not in the balls of B. Again this is a ⪆ 1
refinement.
   In the sequel, we will use the following properties of the shading (FB , YB ):
       • λ(FB , YB ) ⪆ δ η for each B ∈ B.
       • |YB (FB )| is roughly the same for all FB ∈ FB .
       • If Y (F ) ∩ B is non-empty, then F ∈ F(FB ) for some FB ∈ FB and Y (F ) ∩ B ⊂
         YB (FB ).
       • The multiplicity |F(FB )Y (x)| is roughly constant for x ∈ YB (FB ).
  Now we apply the biased maximal density factoring, Lemma 16.7, to FB , and
we let WB be the resulting family of convex sets (the maximal density factoring
replaces FB by a large subset, but we will abuse notation and continue to refer to
this set as FB ; this is harmless because of all the uniformity in the bullet points
above). By pigeonholing, we can suppose that for each B, the convex sets W ∈ WB
             THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                                  137

have dimensions a × b × r1⃗1, with a ≤ b ≤ r1 . Thus for each ball B we obtain a
decomposition
                                       G
                                FB =       FB,W .
                                              W ∈WB

   The way we proceed depends on the dimensions of W . We let τ > 0 be a new small
parameter, to be chosen later. If a ≥ δ 1−τ , then we are in the thick case. In this case
we will show that U (F, Y ) is large by proving (16.27). If a ≤ δ 1−τ , then we are in the
thin case. This case divides into several subcases. In the most important subcase, we
will estimate µ(WB ) by an induction on scales. We deal with these cases in the next
two sections.

16.5.1. Slab case. Recall that r1 = δ εscal , where εscal > 0 is the constant in Lemma 16.1,
which is a tiny constant that we get to choose. We define the slab case to be the
case b ≥ δ εscal r1 = δ 2εscal . In this case, W is a giant slab of dimensions at least
a × δ 2εscal × δ εscal × δ εscal .

  Applying Lemma 13.9 to (W′B , YW′B ), we have µ(W′B , YW′B ) ≲ δ −Cn (η+εscal +ηbias ) for
some constant Cn′ depending only on n, i.e.,
                                                  ′

Combining this with (16.35), we have
                                              ′

   Next we estimate ∆max (FB ). Each flake segment FB ∈ FB is contained in a flake

r1−2 δ −η . Using this, we can see that
                    |W′B ||W | ⪆ ∆max (FB )−1 |FB ||FB | ≳ r12 δ η |FB ||FB |.
