                                       17. Applications

17.1. Rogers-type (n − 2)-plane Kakeya sets in Rn . In this section, we apply
Theorem 1.1 to a codimension-two analogue of the planar variant of the Kakeya
problem introduced by Rogers [24]. This gives a full-dimensional result for suitable
two-dimensional families of (n − 2)-planes in Rn . When n = 4, this coincides exactly
with the “real” 2-plane problem considered by Rogers.
   Let M ⊂ Gr(n, n − 2) be (a compact patch of) a smooth two-dimensional mani-
fold, equipped with the normalized measure µ induced by the major-angle metric on
Gr(n, n − 2). Recall from Definition 3.4 that
                                    ∠(V, W ) = ∥πV − πW ∥.

  For an (n − 1)-dimensional linear subspace Π ⊂ Rn and a > 0, define
                                                                             
                                
                                                                             
                                                                              
                      MaΠ := V ∈ M :              inf        ∠(V, W ) < a .
                                              W ∈Gr(n,n−2)                   
                                                  W ⊂Π

  Also, for V ∈ M and b > 0, write
                            Bb (V ) := {W ∈ M : ∠(V, W ) < b}.

  Recall that Rogers’ curvature condition for (n − 2)-dimensional families N of 2-
planes in Rn is                           
                         µ NΠε1m ∩ Bε2 (V ) ≲ εn−m
                                               1   εm−2
                                                    2   ,
for every Πm ∈ Gr(n, m) with 3 ≤ m ≤ n − 1, every V ∈ N , and every ε1 , ε2 > 0.
  The natural analogue for families of k-planes is obtained by replacing the exponent
m − 2 by m − k:                            
                         µ MεΠ1m ∩ Bε2 (V ) ≲ εn−m1   εm−k
                                                       2   ,
144        CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

for every Πm ∈ Gr(n, m) with k + 1 ≤ m ≤ n − 1, every V ∈ M, and every ε1 , ε2 > 0.
In the codimension-two case k = n − 2, the only intermediate dimension is m = n − 1,
and the corresponding condition reduces to
                                                   
                                  µ MaΠ ∩ Bb (V ) ≲ ab,
for every Π ∈ Gr(n, n − 1), every V ∈ M, and every a, b > 0.
  We shall say that a smooth two-dimensional manifold M ⊂ Gr(n, n−2) is codimension-
two curved in the sense of Rogers if it satisfies this condition. When n = 4, this is
precisely Rogers’ original curvature condition for the real 2-plane problem in R4 .
  We now show that this curvature condition implies the convex nonconcentration
condition used throughout this paper.
Lemma 17.1. Let M ⊂ Gr(n, n − 2) be a smooth two-dimensional manifold which
is codimension-two curved in the sense of Rogers. Let Λ ⊂ M be a δ-separated set,
and for each V ∈ Λ, let FV be an arbitrary δ-flake with dir(FV ) = V . Set
                                   F := {FV : V ∈ Λ}.
Then
                                       ∆max (F) ≲ 1,
where the implicit constant is allowed to depend on M. In particular,
                                       ∆max (F) ≤ δ −η
for every fixed η > 0 and all sufficiently small δ.

Proof. Fix a convex set K ⊂ Rn . We need to prove
                                              F ∈F[K] |F |
                                            P
                               ∆(F, K) =                     ≲ 1.
                                                 |K|
This is trivial if F[K] = ∅, so suppose that K contains at least one δ-flake from
F. Let a1 ≤ a2 ≤ · · · ≤ an be the dimensions of K, in the sense of the axes of its
John ellipsoid, and let e1 , . . . , en be the corresponding orthogonal principal directions.
Since K contains a δ-flake, we have a1 , a2 ≳ δ and a3 , . . . , an ≳ 1.
  Set a := a1 and b := a2 . Define
                               Π := e⊥
                                     1 = span(e2 , . . . , en ),

and
                                 VK := span(e3 , . . . , en ).
We claim that every FV ∈ F[K] satisfies V ∈ MCa     Π and ∠(V, VK ) ≲ b. To see this,
first note that the unit ball in V is contained in the difference set FV − FV , while
                                   FV − FV ⊂ K − K.
Since the widths of K − K in the e1 and e2 directions are O(a) and O(b), respectively,
it follows that
                            |v · e1 | ≲ a,   |v · e2 | ≲ b,
              THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                      145

for every unit vector v ∈ V . The first estimate implies that V is within major angle
O(a) of an (n − 2)-plane contained in Π (by Lemma 3.13, just consider the (n − 2)-
plane πΠ (V )). The two estimates together give ∥πVK⊥ |V ∥ ≲ b, which by Item (i) of
Theorem 3.1 gives
                                    ∠(V, VK ) ≲ b.
   Note that the curvature condition requires the ball Bb (·) to be centered at an
element of M, whereas VK need not belong to M. This issue is easily fixed by
choosing a flake FV0 ∈ F[K]. Then, V0 ∈ Λ ⊂ M and the preceding estimate gives
                                     ∠(V0 , VK ) ≲ b.
 Consequently, for every FV ∈ F[K], the triangle inequality for the major-angle
metric gives
                     ∠(V, V0 ) ≤ ∠(V, VK ) + ∠(VK , V0 ) ≲ b.
Combining this with V ∈ MCa
                         Π , we obtain

                       {dir(F ) : F ∈ F[K]} ⊂ MCa
                                               Π ∩ BCb (V0 ).

  We next discretize the codimension-two curvature condition. Since M is a smooth

                                                         ′

                                         −2
                 # Λ ∩ MCa                   C a

by a standard packing argument (since a, b ≳ δ, the O(δ) losses arising from the
packing argument can be absorbed into constant multiples of a and b) for a slightly
bigger constant C ′ > 0. Next, applying the curvature condition, we conclude that
                                    #F[K] ≲ abδ −2 .
Finally, |F | ∼ δ 2 for every F ∈ F, while John’s theorem gives |K| ≈ a1 a2 . . . an ≳ ab.
Therefore, we get
                                     #F[K]|F |     abδ −2 δ 2
                          ∆(F, K) =             ≲             ≲ 1.

Since K was arbitrary, we can conclude that
                                     ∆max (F) ≲ 1,
as desired.                                                                             □

  We shall now resolve the “real” 2-plane problem posed by Rogers. In what follows,
we will use Hs (E) to denote the s-dimensional Hausdorff measure of a set E.
Theorem 17.2 (Rogers-type (n−2)-plane Kakeya problem in Rn ). Let M ⊂ Gr(n, n − 2)
be a smooth two-dimensional manifold which is codimension-two curved in the sense
of Rogers, and suppose that E ⊂ Rn contains a translate of every plane V ∈ M.
Then,
                                 dimH (E) = n.
In particular, when n = 4, every real K24 -set in the sense of Rogers [24] has full
Hausdorff dimension.
146          CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

Proof. Suppose for a contradiction that
                                         dimH (E) < s < n.
Fix 0 < β < n − s, and let η > 0 be given by Theorem 1.1 with ε = β therein.
  Since dimH (E) < s, we have Hs (E) = 0. Hence, for every ε > 0 and every δ0 > 0,
we may cover E by balls
                                     [
                               E ⊂ B(xj , rj ),

                                                         rjs < ε.

                                           λ(F, Y ) ≳ k −2 .

                                               k −2 ≳ δ η ,
for sufficiently large k. Thus, (F, Y ) satisfies the hypotheses of Theorem 1.1. There-

                                 |U (F, Y )| ≳ δ β |F||FV |.
Using |F| = |Λ| ≳ k −2 δ −2 and |FV | ∼ δ 2 , this gives
                                         |U (F, Y )| ≳ k −2 δ β .
On the other hand,
                                        U (F, Y ) ⊂ Nc0 δ (Ek ).
If Nk := |Bk |, then Ek is a union of Nk balls of radius comparable to δ, and hence
                                         |Nc0 δ (Ek )| ≲ Nk δ n .
Consequently,
                                           Nk δ n ≳ k −2 δ β ,
or equivalently,
                                         Nk δ s ≳ k −2 δ s+β−n .
Since δ = 2−k and s + β − n < 0, the right-hand side tends to infinity as k → ∞.
  However, every ball in Bk has radius comparable to δ, so the choice of the cover of
E gives
                             Nk δ s ≲         rjs < ε.
                                         X

                                               B(xj ,rj )∈Bk
148        CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG

  Since k ≥ k0 and k0 → ∞ as δ0 → 0, choosing δ0 sufficiently small ensures that
the preceding lower bound for Nk δ s exceeds this upper bound Nk δ s ≲ ε, giving a
contradiction. Therefore,
                                  dimH (E) ≥ s.
Since this holds for every s < n, the conclusion follows.                              □

   One can easily see from the proof of Theorem 17.2 that we still have full Hausdorff
dimension even if we only take a patch of each plane with positive Hn−2 -measure
instead of the whole plane. Such a “compact” version is closer to the classical formu-
lation of the Kakeya set conjecture.
   It is also worth pointing out that in [24], Rogers proved that every real K24 -set has
Hausdorff dimension ≥ 7/2 by adapting Wolff’s hairbrush argument [31] to 2-planes in
R4 . His proof actually yields something stronger, and may be used to obtain the cor-
responding maximal function estimates. Thus, Rogers’ approach provides additional
information beyond the Hausdorff dimension statement and remains particularly rel-
evant from the perspective of maximal function estimates. It is a good question how
to obtain the best possible such estimates, which may require the resolution of the
Kakeya maximal conjecture for tubes in R3 .

17.2. Kakeya for a × b × ⃗1 planks in Rn . Before ending this paper, we point out
that good Kakeya estimates for a × b × ⃗1 planks in B1 ⊂ Rn (n ≥ 3) can also be
obtained as by-products of the proof of Theorem 1.1. For example, since the assertion
KKT (0) in Definition 12.1 holds, by Lemma 13.1, we immediately get the following
result:
Theorem 17.3. For every ε > 0, there exists η, a0 > 0 such that the following
holds whenever 0 < a ≤ a0 and a ≤ b ≤ 1. Let L be a set of a × b × ⃗1 planks in
B1 ⊂ Rn with ∆max (L) ≤ a−η , and let Y be a shading on L with λ(L, Y ) ≥ aη . Then
|U (L, Y )| ≥ aε |L||L|.

   Note that Theorem 17.3 includes Theorem 1.1 as a special case by taking a = b = δ.
We can also require the planks to be Frostman instead of Katz–Tao. More precisely,
since the assertion KF (0) in Definition 12.2 holds, by Lemma 13.4, we have:
Theorem 17.4. For every ε > 0, there exists η, a0 > 0 such that the following holds
whenever 0 < a ≤ a0 and a ≤ b ≤ 1. Let L be a set of a×b× ⃗1 planks in B1 ⊂ Rn with
CF (L) ≤ a−η , and let Y be a shading on L with λ(L, Y ) ≥ aη . Then |U (L, Y )| ≥ aε .

  Theorem 17.4 is a higher-dimensional generalization of Lemma 9.6. Note that if
we take a = b = δ, then Theorem 17.4 can be viewed as a “Frostman” version of
Theorem 1.1.
Remark 17.5. The ranges for a and b in Theorem 17.3 and 17.4 are initially 0 < a ≤
b ≤ b0 for some b0 depending on ε. However, similar arguments as in Remark 9.7
remove the smallness restriction on b from both of them.
              THE KAKEYA PROBLEM FOR CODIMENSION-TWO PLANES IN Rn                         149

  In our proof of the (n − 2)-plane sticky Kakeya in Rn , Lemma 9.6 is the key
ingredient for growing medium grains in each slice. It is unclear to the authors
whether Theorem 17.3 or 17.4 could be a useful input in other problems.

                         Appendix A. Probability lemmas

  In this appendix, we use simple probabilistic lemmas to prove Lemmas 11.6, 11.8,
and 12.5. These lemmas are basically different versions of Chernoff’s inequality, and
a detailed proof can be found in [14] and [5]. Here we only record the statements for
the reader’s convenience. We use P[·] to denote the probability of an event.

Lemma A.1 ([14, Lemma A.1]). Suppose that X1 , . . . , XN are i.i.d. random vari-
ables. Suppose that 0 ≤ X1 ≤ M almost surely and that m = EX1 . If N m ≥ M ,
then for every S > 0,
                                                              S
                           P[X1 + · · · + XN > S] ≤ e10 e− N m .
If N m ≤ M , then for every S > 0,
                                                              S
                            P[X1 + · · · + XN > S] ≤ e10 e− 2M .

Lemma A.2 (Chernoff [5]). Let X1 , . . . , XN be independent random variables taking
value 1 with probability p and value 0 with probability 1 − p. Let A ≥ 2. Then
      P[X1 + · · · + XN ≤ pN/2] ≤ e−pN/8 ;      P[X1 + · · · + XN ≥ ApN ] ≤ e−ApN/6 .

  We will consider random translations and rigid motions of Rn . Recall that for
v ∈ Rn , we defined Rv (x) = x + v. A random translation of size ≤ ρ is a translation
Rv by a vector v ∈ B(0, ρ) selected randomly from the uniform measure on B(0, ρ).
A random rigid motion of size ≤ 1 is a random rotation composed with a random
translation of size ≤ 1.
  First, we state and prove a more general version of Lemma 11.6.

                                   2 −(n−k) C (F)−1 /10                     2
                  1 − 2e−(log(1/δ)) δ        F
                                                          ≥ 1 − 2e−(log(1/δ)) /C
(as CF (F) ≲ δ −(n−k) ), we have |F′ | ∼ (log(1/δ))2 δ −(n−k) CF (F)−1 . In particular, |F′ | ≈
δ −(n−k) CF (F)−1 .
    Next we turn to bound CF (F′ ). Let K ⊂ B1 be a convex set of dimensions a1 ×
· · · × an−k × 1 × · · · × 1 with δ ≤ a1 ≤ · · · ≤ an−k ≤ 1. Applying Lemma A.1 to those
Xj ’s associated to F[K], we know that
                                                               S
                                P[|F′ [K]| > S] ≤ e10 e− 2M ,
where M = ⌈|F[K]| EX1 ⌉ = ⌈|F[K]| p⌉. In particular, by taking S = AM , we get
                            h                             i
                          P |F′ [K]| > A⌈|F[K]| p⌉ ≤ e10 e−A/2 .
Since there are only δ −O(1) essentially different choices for the convex set K ⊂ B1 ,
with probability at least 1 − δ −O(1) e10 e−A/2 , we have that |F′ [K]| ≤ A⌈|F[K]| p⌉ for
152          CHUN-KIT LAI, YIXUAN PANG, RAHUL SETHI, AND CHENJIAN WANG
                                                                                  2
every K. We can choose A ⪅ 1 so that the probability is at least 1 − δ 100n for δ
sufficiently small.
  Recall that |F[K]| ≤ CF (F)|K||F|. Then with high probability, for every K, we
have
         |F′ [K]| ⪅ ⌈|F[K]| p⌉ ≤ ⌈CF (F)|K||F| p⌉ = ⌈(log(1/δ))2 δ −(n−k) |K|⌉.
If F′ [K] = ∅, then |F′ [K]| ⪅ CF (F)|K||F′ | trivially holds. Otherwise, we must
have |K| ≥ |F | ∼ δ n−k , which means (log(1/δ))2 δ −(n−k) |K| ≥ 1, and so |F′ [K]| ⪅
(log(1/δ))2 δ −(n−k) |K|. Finally, recall that with high probability
                          |F′ | ∼ (log(1/δ))2 δ −(n−k) CF (F)−1 ,
so we can conclude that |F′ [K]| ⪅ CF (F)|K||F′ |. In particular, CF (F′ ) ⪅ CF (F).   □

  Finally, we state and prove a more general version of Lemma 12.5:

Lemma A.5. Suppose that F is a set of essentially distinct (δ, k)-flakes in B1 ⊂ Rn .
Let R1 , . . . , RJ be independent random rigid motions of size ≤ 1, with J = ⌈CF (F)⌉,
and let F′ = Jj=1 Rj (F). Then with high probability the flakes of F′ are essentially
                  F

distinct up to multiplicity ⪅ 1 and also CF (F′ ) ⪅ 1. After refining F′ to make the
flakes essentially distinct, we have |F′ | ≈ CF (F)|F| and CF (F′ ) ⪅ 1.

  The proof ideas are the same as for the previous Lemma A.3.

Proof. We follow the arguments in [14, Appendix A.1]. Although F is essentially
distinct, F′ may not be. However, we will show that with high probability, for any
(δ, k)-flake F0 , there are ⪅ 1 flakes of F′ contained in 100F0 .
   We define random variables Xj := |Rj (F)[100F0 ]|, where j = 1, . . . , J. Note that
|F [100F0 ]| = Jj=1 Xj .
  ′            P

  Since the flakes of F are essentially distinct, we have Xj = |F[Rj−1 (100F0 )]| ≲ 1. In
fact, there exists M ≲ 1 so that 0 ≤ Xj ≤ M for each j.
  Next, we estimate EXj . By linearity of expectation, we have
                                    X

                                    F ∈F

Since Rj is a random rigid motion, we note that for each F ∈ F,
                             P[Rj (F ) ⊂ 100F0 ] ≲ |Fδ |k+1 .
Therefore,
                                m = EXj ≲ |F||Fδ |k+1 .

 We claim that Jm ≲ 1. To see this, first note that Jm ≲ CF (F)|F||Fδ |k+1 . Suppose
∆max (F) ∼ ∆(F, K) for some convex set K ⊂ B1 , then we have
                                        ∆(F, K)     |F[K]|

Thus by possibly enlarging the previous M , we also have Jm ≤ M for M ≲ 1.
  Therefore, if S ≥ 1, then Lemma A.1 gives
                                                                 S

for some M ≲ 1. Since the number of essentially distinct F0 is at most δ −O(1) , we can
choose S ⪅ 1 so that with high probability |F′ [100F0 ]| ≤ S for every F0 .
   If we refine F′ to be a set of essentially distinct flakes, then we still have |F′ | ≈
CF (F)|F|.
   Now we estimate CF (F′ ). Refining F′ to make the flakes essentially distinct will
only affect CF (F′ ) by a factor ⪅ 1, so we can ignore the refinement.
   Let K ⊂ B2 be a convex set of dimensions a1 × · · · × an−k × 1 × · · · × 1 with

                               Declaration of AI usage

  During the preparation of this manuscript, the authors made limited use of AI-
assisted tools (OpenAI’s ChatGPT-5.5) for auxiliary purposes, namely to assist with
sorting out preliminary literature and searching existing geometric and combinatorial
results (Theorem 3.1 and Lemma 8.9). All references identified through such tools
were independently checked by the authors. All outputs were reviewed and verified
by the author. Except for the uses described above, the formulation of the results, the
proofs, the mathematical arguments, and the final exposition are entirely the work of
the authors. OpenAI’s ChatGPT-6.1 Sol was used at a later stage for language editing
and manuscript consistency checking. The authors accept full responsibility for the
originality, correctness, and integrity of all content contained in this manuscript.
